L., and Holmes, E. C., 2017, “Predicting Virus Emergence amid Evolutionary Noise,” *Open Biology*, 7(10):170189. |
| 1\. Estimating numbers of species on Earth is difficult, requiring assumptions and extrapolations. This table follows Larsen et al. (“Inordinate Fondness”) for animals, plants, fungi, and bacteria, totaling 2.238 billion species. A recent estimate for archaea of 20,000 species (Tahon, G., Geesink, P., and Ettema, T. J., “Expanding Archaeal Diversity and Phylogeny: Past, Present, and Future,” *Annual Review of Microbiology*, 75 \[2021\]:359–81) does not add significantly to the estimate for bacteria alone. The paper by Locey and Lennon (“Scaling Laws”) predicts a much higher number of roughly 1 trillion microbial species alone (including bacteria, archaea, and microscopic fungi), estimates not shown in this table, using a scaling approach. Despite differences in estimates, a common finding is that microbes vastly outnumber animals, plants, and nonmicroscopic fungi. |
| 2\. There are no suitable estimates for numbers of species for all viruses as a group. The International Committee on Taxonomy of Viruses currently recognizes 6,950 species (Lefkowitz et al., “Virus Taxonomy”); however, most virus diversity remains undescribed. Rohwer (“Global Phage Diversity”) estimated roughly 100 million species for phage viruses, a virus group that infects bacteria and archaea. Geoghegan and Holmes (“Predicting Virus Emergence”) estimate 87 million virus species infecting eukaryote species. Locey and Lennon (“Scaling Laws”) predict 100 billion “microbial” species (bacteria, archaea, and microscopic fungi only, excluding viruses or microscopic eukaryotes) using scaling laws. |

Scientific advances in resolving life’s evolutionary story have been accompanied at nearly every step by discovery of additional complexity. Our understanding of the network of life will always be a work in progress. Because our data and analyses are improving. And because the network is dynamic, growing and changing, even as you read this. New lineages are always emerging or disappearing, and some genes are always being moved somewhere. This is vastly more common among viruses and bacteria than among vertebrates, but still the network evolves. Most nascent organismal lineages and genetic transfers are likely to be ephemeral, but some may last, either by chance or by natural selection favoring adaptation.

The new narrative uses the metaphors of networks or webs as patterns of relationship because these are more appropriate for describing evolution than that of strictly branching trees. Analyses and description of phylogenetic trees are more appropriate for some species (most eukaryotes) and less so for others (bacteria and archaea), where horizontal evolution is more common. Evolutionary trees will always be useful and are deeply embedded in evolutionary discourse. But for overarching narratives, networks that combine vertical and horizontal evolution are much more accurate. As I will discuss later, the new narrative also better informs thinking about the environment, health, and conservation. A variety of popular misconceptions related to evolution and phylogeny are outlined in [table 1.4](#08_ch1.xhtml#tab1-4). Their corresponding “better conceptions” often reflect evolution’s new narrative as well.

Some will object to elevating the importance of horizontal evolution as an explanation and driver of life’s diversification. I understand the reluctance, but suspect it reflects a primary focus on groups such as vertebrates, where horizontal evolution is rare. It helps to note that frequency and importance (or consequence) are not the same. The horizontal evolution events early in life’s history (entities joining) associated with the emergence of eukaryotes have had greater consequences for diversifying life than vertical evolution (entities splitting) among vertebrates alone. Elevating horizontal evolution, however, does not diminish the importance of vertical evolution. Horizontal evolution is often most reliably identified against a backdrop of vertical evolution. The two are complementary in revealing the complex patterns of relationship among life-forms.

| Table 1.4. Common myths about evolution, phylogenetic trees, and life |
| --- |
| Misconception || Better Conception |
| --- | --- |
| Phylogenetic trees and rooted networks show evolutionary progress, usually advancing from species on the left to those on the right || The ordering of tips has no meaning regarding progress; all tips denoting extant species are of equal age relative to shared common ancestors |
| Species arise by splitting only, with one becoming two || Species can also merge, with two becoming one |
| Some living species are ancestors of other living species || Living species are cousins, not ancestors |
| Species are consistently, clearly defined || In many cases, designating species is difficult |
| Evolution progresses from primitive forms to advanced || Evolution can yield both increased and decreased complexity |
| We’ve discovered most or all of Earth’s unique life-forms (species of organisms) || The rate of discovery of new life-forms and variants is as high as ever, in large part because of molecular sequencing efforts |
| Viruses are not alive || Viruses reproduce, carry genetic instructions, and evolve, and many biologists do consider viruses as alive or in a grey zone; life is a process (see [chapter 4](#11_ch4.xhtml)) |
| Inheritance of acquired features doesn’t exist || Inheritance of acquired features is integral to horizontal evolution and, to a lesser degree, immune system evolution; the term carries historical baggage |
| All organisms including humans are intelligently designed || Organisms change over time by processes of evolution and often include inefficient features |

The methods of phylogenetics for estimating genealogy remain vital as they are needed to evaluate vertical evolution and, in doing so, to expose horizontal evolution. The tangled networks of relationships occur at several levels of biological organization: among species, among individual organisms, and among their genes and entire genomes. Though recovering these networks of relationships requires increasingly large datasets (mostly molecular) and analytical tools, the findings explain more than relationships alone. They map the ancient and ongoing integration among life-forms.

Finally, I want to call attention to two more differences in the implications of the new and conventional narratives for life’s evolution (see [table 1.2](#08_ch1.xhtml#tab1-2)). First, recognizing the importance of horizontal evolution places new focus on the episodic nature of evolution’s tempo. Events of joining and horizontal gene transfer are relatively sudden compared with the conventional scenario of more constant, smaller-scale, single-DNA-base substitutions. Second, horizontal evolution entails inheritance of characteristics acquired during an individual’s lifetime. Horizontal gene transfer into a single bacterium can be inherited by its progeny. This is reminiscent of, but quite different from, the older historical conception of Lamarckian inheritance, whereby physical traits of organisms acquired during their lifetimes could be inherited by their progeny. Genes and their mechanisms of inheritance were unknown when inheritance of acquired traits was entertained by biologists like Lamarck and Darwin. Nor was there any detailed understanding of key differences between body cells (soma) and reproductive cells (sperm, eggs). Regardless of what we call it, the reality of many acquired traits becoming heritable is now clear. This stems not just from the mechanisms of hybridization, joining, and horizontal gene transfer discussed here, but from mechanisms associated with epigenetics (changes in the expression levels of genes), immune system capacity, and genome editing as done by organisms themselves.^([14](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter1-14)) Though detailed review of the findings from each of these fields of study is beyond the scope of this book, they fit better with the new than with the conventional narrative for evolution.

### Why Has It Taken So Long?

Why has it has taken so long for evolution’s new narrative to gain traction among nonspecialists, the public, and even many biologists? Key discoveries about horizontal evolution have been hiding in plain sight for a very long time. Darwin knew of exceptions to the metaphor of a “great tree” for the evolution of different life-forms, even as he first applied the term. Animal breeders, like Darwin the pigeon fancier, and avid gardeners all knew that hybridizing among species could lead to new forms. Similarly, many modern biologists have seen through their own research that the tree metaphor and accompanying narrative of serial branching are insufficient or even misleading as descriptors of evolution. My arguments here are, in many ways, a rear-guard and after-the-facts action to address the narrative.

There are of course many reasons for the delay. Some likely reasons stem from differences in the timing and pace of scientific advances across different disciplines. Still other reasons concern obstacles to cultural change, inside and outside scientific communities. Perhaps the enduring fact of common ancestry for life may have helped to shield an outdated narrative. For the first century after Darwin and Wallace, most evolutionists studied animals and plants, and the mechanisms of change seen in them were taken as examples that could be extrapolated for the evolution of all life-forms. But, in hindsight, that was mistaken. The great diversity and ubiquity of microscopic, single-celled organisms remained to be discovered.

We also like to see ourselves as the star of any story that includes us. But consider how far removed our species is from an average, representative species of life’s diversity. We are one among millions if not billions of species, depending on how species are defined, and very few are like us. Even the complete set of vertebrate animal species, about 66,000 strong, represents a paltry fraction of life’s diversity. The vast majority of species and organisms, now and across time, are single-celled microbes. And it is their modes of change and proliferation, varied as they are, that dominate by frequency across the 3.8-billion-year history of life.

The attention we humans give to microbes, even now, is still largely focused on our relationships with pathogens or species used in industry. The vast majority of microbes are unseen and unnamed. Out of sight and out of mind. This is despite the fact that microbes pioneered in oxygenating the Earth’s atmosphere, making it habitable for our kind, and in evolving the fundamental molecular and genetic mechanisms that facilitate all lives. This includes replicating DNAs and assembling proteins from them. We may be standing on the shoulders of giants for our scientific progress, as Newton pointed out, but we are standing on the shoulders of prodigious hordes of tiny, unheralded microbes for our evolved abilities to live and pass on our genes.

I think the general invisibility of this heroic role for single-celled organisms underlies a lack of appreciation for their frequent and consequential horizontal evolution. Hence, their influence on the evolutionary narrative has been lagging. Opinions about narratives are often subjective, even among scientists, reflecting their particular, often narrow interests. If a narrative works for the restricted set of organisms they study, there may be no hurry to change, and the conventional narrative has sufficed for biologists studying humans and other animals until relatively recently.

Two additional factors contributed to the delay. The conventional pattern of branching among species, with one species giving rise to two, was (and often still is) the only option available in analyses by computer algorithms for estimating and diagramming the relationships among species. Only branching trees could be produced, not networks. Simultaneous evaluation of both vertical and horizontal relationships for species and genes is complicated, and development of computer methods for this approach is relatively recent. Equally important, branching trees provided the relatedness patterns among species as a hierarchy, precisely as needed by biologists creating classification systems. Species could be grouped within genera, genera within families, and so on, for orders, classes, and kingdoms. Phylogenetic networks are less easily converted to classifications of organisms, as movement of genetic materials among distant relatives can obscure hierarchical relationships.

The new narrative is a reenvisioning of existing knowledge, shining a brighter light on the mechanisms and facts of genetic integration among species of organisms. The new narrative better frames our thinking. It better serves our imagination regarding life’s story and life’s potential. This potential includes our human future. Narratives, at their best, can free our minds to see beyond minutia, to extrapolate, and to inspire.

The opening lines of Emily Dickinson’s poem “The Brain Is Wider than the Sky” points to the capacity of our minds to comprehend things whose dimensions are out of scale with our own lives.

> The Brain—is wider than the Sky—
> 
> For—put them side by side—
> 
> The one the other will contain
> 
> With ease—and You—beside—^([15](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter1-15))

For our purposes, this comprehension includes things that are real but hard to see like geologic time spans and the many natural forces that play out across them, including horizontal evolution, molecular mutation, natural selection, and integration among species. Evolution’s new narrative can enhance our view of how evolution has happened and how it might happen in the future.

### The Road Ahead

An overview of topics and chapters for the remainder of the book is as follows. [Chapters 2 through 4](#09_ch2.xhtml) compose [part 1](#07_pt1.xhtml), and will establish the framework for the new narrative of evolution. In [chapter 2](#09_ch2.xhtml), I place the new narrative about life’s diversification into the context of centralized (treelike) and decentralized (networked) systems in general and the network of life in particular. This entails synthesis of life’s genealogy and the relative roles of centralized and decentralized inheritance within the primary groups (Bacteria, Archaea, Eukarya). The concepts of centralization and decentralization have been considered to be important in understanding complex systems in governance, economics, financial markets, information technology, and social and cultural evolution. However, they have been little considered by evolutionary biologists, and the potential for new insights regarding their relative strengths and weaknesses in the evolution of biodiversity are considered.

[Chapter 3](#10_ch3.xhtml) explores the early history of discoveries relating to the mechanisms of horizontal evolution and decentralization that support the new evolutionary narrative. This chapter will dive into the processes of material horizontal evolution: hybridization among species, merger or joining, and horizontal gene transfer. I highlight the mismatch between the implications of the discoveries and the conventional narrative for evolution.

[Chapter 4](#11_ch4.xhtml) explores the difficulty of defining, or the fuzzy boundaries of, some entities at the heart of life’s narrative, such as individuals, species, viruses, and life itself. Recent research shows viruses in particular to have a major role in life’s horizontal evolution, and new knowledge of virus natural history is leading biologists to reconsider the integration between living and nonliving systems and the status of viruses as a part of life’s network. Current understanding of all these entities attests further to the theme of life’s deep and recurrent integration.

Having established the framework for this new narrative, [part II](#12_pt2.xhtml) explores why developing a new narrative for evolution matters. [Chapters 5](#13_ch5.xhtml) and [6](#14_ch6.xhtml) focus on the new narrative’s implications for understanding human health and environmental needs, respectively. These chapters extend the concept of species integration from genealogy to natural selection and coevolution, the heritable change that species impose on each other over time. Human and environmental health are deeply influenced by, and have coevolved with, the activities of other life-forms.

[Chapter 7](#15_ch7.xhtml) provides an outlook on the future of biodiversity. I discuss current status for a variety of species, where data are sufficient, as well as how some species are adapting to human-induced environmental change. We are witnessing losses and redistribution of biological diversity, as well as a surprising level of gains via hybridization associated with climate change. I explore the role of horizontal evolution in human hands, as applied to conservation of species, and developments in the relatively new endeavor of synthetic life, including creating new life-forms, mostly microbial, and extensively editing the genomes of others.

In [chapter 8](#16_ch8.xhtml), I explore the future of our own species in the context of technology. I examine how technology augments our bodies, enhances life-spans, and dramatically extends our brainpower. I argue that human technology has strong similarities to living systems, and I make the case that humans are coevolving with technology. This cultural coevolution is in keeping with evolution’s new narrative, as cultural evolution, driven by sharing (teaching and learning) among groups, is also horizontal evolution. Knowledge of the mechanisms and consequences of horizontal evolution will provide practical lessons, as we navigate the health and environmental challenges ahead.

## 2 Decentralization and Innovation

> Decentralization can be seen as a form of anti-fragility: a system that is not only resilient to shocks and disruptions but thrives on them.
> 
> —ARVIND NARAYANAN ET AL., *BITCOIN AND CRYPTOCURRENCY TECHNOLOGIES*

As successful systems age, they risk obsolescence. Once new and daring, their ideas and implementation may become rigid and inefficient, falling out of sync with changing conditions. This can happen in systems of government, business, communication, and countless others, where the controlling factions and features fail to innovate and adapt over time. As Matt Ridley, author of *How Innovation Works*, notes, “One of the peculiar features of history is that empires are bad at innovation. Though they have wealthy and educated elites, imperial regimes tend to preside over gradual declines in inventiveness, which contribute to their eventual undoing. The Egyptian, Persian, Roman, Byzantine, Han Aztec, Inca, Hapsburg, Ming, Ottoman, Russian and British empires all bear this out.” In contrast, “Italy’s most fertile inventive period was in the Renaissance, when it was the small city states, run by merchants, that drove innovation: in Genoa, Florence, Venice, Luca, Siena and Milan. Fragmented polities proved better than united ones. Ancient Greece teaches the same lesson.”^([1](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-1))

Failing systems can be rescued, if not buried, by a more decentralized and innovative approach. Decentralization of key controls, often unwelcome by entrenched powers, can bypass the controlling powers, fostering new ideas and vigor. Johannes Gutenburg’s mid-fifteenth-century printing of the Bible in Latin is an example. He removed sole access to holy documents from clergymen and scholars of Hebrew and Greek, and provided it to lay readers. In the same vein, the printing and spread of Martin Luther’s “95-Theses” in the early sixteenth century, led to further decentralizing of the Church’s power, contributing, in turn, to the Protestant Reformation.

Democracy is another innovative and decentralizing force. Having started in ancient Greece by allowing all citizens to speak and vote in legislatures, this began an approach intending to better serve the interests of human populations globally. It can remove or weaken control by authoritarian groups. Programs in support of education, health, and business can thrive with support and funding from the public. However, democracy as a social innovation depends entirely on the rule of law, and is vulnerable to attack by those seeking power by other means.

Perhaps more often, decentralization stems from technological innovation. During the past century, innovations in health care, energy production, agriculture, commerce, and transportation have contributed to dramatic improvements in human living standards. Improvements range from reduced infant mortality, longer lives, and better health to less poverty, and greater access to clean water and comfortable housing. This is not to say the current situation is sufficient, just better than it had been.

Vaccination is a changing set of technological innovations that has saved millions of lives over the course of 450 years. A report from China in the 1570s describes people having trace amounts of human smallpox scabs put in their noses as a protection against the disease. Similar methods were applied in Turkey and India in the early and middle eighteenth century, with variation in their methods of inoculate preparation, storage, and delivery. Vaccination against smallpox using cowpox scabs became widespread after Edward Jenner’s experiments and publication of 1798. Indeed, the English word “vaccination” derives from the Latin *vaccinus*, from cows. This crazy-sounding innovation—infect yourself to protect yourself—was certainly discovered and altered intentionally and by accident in multiple locations at different times by different people, before being refined and passed on as folk wisdom, all long before its science was understood.

The most recent vaccine innovations have built on the early use of scabs and pus containing viruses, with thousands of modern innovations allowing the molecular production and delivery of the exact gene snippets from viruses that are key for development of immunity. Relevant innovations include electricity, computer chips and software for data analyses, and all the specialized equipment and ideas applied to making specific genes from chemicals. The set of novel vaccines most widely used against COVID-19 is the result of a torrid lab race among hundreds of groups of scientists from around the world, experimenting with the best ideas, both old and new, that they can find. A widely used single-shot vaccine uses a harmless virus as a vehicle to carry an engineered gene that is copied by the recipient’s cells, stimulating antibody production. Two other vaccines deliver engineered messenger RNAs (chemical instructions) directly to cells without any vehicle, which then make virus proteins that stimulate antibody production. These approaches were preceded in the mid-twentieth century by the weakening of viruses by forcing them to grow without key nutrients, prior to using them in vaccines. In these ways and others, the ancient practice of infection for protection has been improved by serial innovations.

Considering an entirely different form of technology, the ubiquitous smartphone is also not a single invention. It is an innovative and dizzying combination of preexisting technologies, including telephones, cameras, clocks, calculators, maps, GPS service, pedometers, blood-pressure monitors, email, texting, social media, touch screens, apps, videos, face recognition, payment systems, and internet searches.

The innovations that have driven civilization, from vaccines to laws, language, telescopes, money, computers, and DNA sequencing are rarely, if ever, implemented from scratch, fully formed. Rather, they result from the mixing of ideas and from serendipity and chance. Ridley, puts it this way: innovation “happens when ideas can meet and mate, when experiment is encouraged, when people and goods can move freely.”^([2](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-2))

As I will argue in this chapter, horizontal evolution is a decentralizing force, and it is adept at experimentation and innovation, where genes and the features they encode move across groups of organisms and meet in new combinations. I begin by considering two nonliving systems where decentralization has been explicitly sought out and implemented as part of the innovation process. This will help us see some of its advantages ([box 2.1](#09_ch2.xhtml#box2-1)). The first system is communications; the second is money. After that, I trace the role of horizontal evolution and decentralization in the evolution of life’s primary groups (Bacteria, Archaea, and Eukarya). Along the way, I review some major innovations in life’s evolution that have been influenced by decentralization owing to horizontal evolution. That discussion follows an evolutionary timeline, proceeding from life’s origins to the innovations in eukaryotes of harvesting oxygen and sunlight, multicellular living, and sexual reproduction. I clarify that the extent of decentralization and centralization of genes varies broadly over time and across species. They exist along a continuum.

### Communications

In 1959, 30-year-old electrical engineer Paul Baran was asked by the RAND Corporation in California to develop a communication system that could survive a nuclear attack. This was a pressing concern during the Cold War era. Conjuring the possibilities for communication networks, he identified three general forms ([figure 2.1](#09_ch2.xhtml#fig2-1)). A fully centralized version was highly vulnerable to attack, having a single hub for communications. A series of hubs, connected to one another by single lines, was decentralized to a degree, but still vulnerable. For Baran, the least vulnerable structure was essentially a mesh—called a distributed network—with enough alternative paths for communication among nodes should some of the nodes be destroyed. His insights and network protocols for distributed communication networks were essentially ignored by the military funders of RAND at the time. However, they were rediscovered independently by the US Advanced Research Projects Agency (ARPA) in the late 1960s as they sought secure connection for computers being used for research in different locations. ARPA’s computer network (ARPANET) ultimately gave rise to what we now call the internet. Its current structure is far from an optimal distributed design; however, some of the network communication protocols first envisioned by Paul Baran were successfully implemented.

![FIGURE 2.1. Three general network forms as identified by Paul Baran in 1959, showing degrees of decentralization, from least on the left to most on the right.](images/Mindell_Evolu_Fig_2_1.png)

FIGURE 2.1. Three general network forms as identified by Paul Baran in 1959, showing degrees of decentralization, from least on the left to most on the right.

The internet innovation has now remade global communications and transactions. About 95 percent of all information existing on the planet is estimated to be digitized, and most of that is accessible on the internet.^([3](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-3)) Newspaper publishers and radio and television broadcasters had served as gatekeepers for the news, opinions, and related information, and only those with control or access could tell the stories they wanted. However, the internet allowed many to bypass the controlling powers of twentieth century. But with decidedly mixed results, as it has spread misinformation and confusion too. Consumers can choose the news they prefer, regardless of it’s veracity. Inevitably, the internet has also led to recentralization of some services, as Amazon, Facebook, and Google dominate aspects of commerce and information services.

Decentralization may resolve some problems while introducing others. And those new problems may require further decentralization. In 2020, sensitive online data were hacked in a single large-scale event and stolen from thousands of organizations around the world, including the US Treasury and Commerce Departments and the US National Telecommunications and Information Administration.^([4](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-4)) A Department of Homeland Security (DHS) spokesperson had noted earlier that “our adversaries exploit a fundamental asymmetry in our network infrastructure: while nearly all of our systems and networks are globally interconnected, our defensive capabilities are not.”^([5](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-5)) In response, the DHS is developing a network of networks to connect US federal, state, and local agencies; law enforcement; and the private companies that run a lot of the nation’s critical cyber infrastructure. Officials refer to this effort as building a cyber immunity system. Decentralized networks require decentralized defenses.

Extending the decentralized approach of the internet to international business and politics, Anne-Marie Slaughter makes the case for the high value of innovative network approaches to problem-solving.^([6](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-6)) She argues that individual countries should not be the sole focus of policy concerns, as if individual countries or institutions are engaged in a one-on-one chess match. Rather, it is the web of connections among countries, their links to one another, that demand our focus in understanding the consequences of policy changes. More links among institutions can provide more resilience with more alternative paths for shared success in case of local problems. In this view, governments, economies, and societies adopting openness, with relationship and policy networks rather than strict hierarchies, will compete and succeed most effectively.

### Money

In 2008, a white paper titled “Bitcoin: A Peer-to-Peer Electronic Cash System” was privately emailed to a small group of cryptographers—people interested in securing communication networks—under the pseudonym Satoshi Nakamoto.^([7](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-7)) This paper resolved a long-standing problem in protecting computer networks from attack, known as the Byzantine generals’ problem. Essentially, the problem is that all computer nodes in a network, like generals in an army, must agree on a concerted strategy, despite the possibility that some of the generals or their messengers might be compromised. The resolution in Nakamoto’s paper involved several innovations in securing of communications including unforgeable digital signatures, large-scale redundancy, and an economic disincentive to attacks called proof of work.

Spurred by the global economic decline of 2007–9 known as the Great Recession, and its root causes in excessive high-risk and undisclosed centralized banking practices in the United States and elsewhere, Bitcoin emerged as a nascent decentralized system of money. In the United States, the Great Recession caused losses in housing prices and stock markets of about $11.5 trillion, and about 8.6 million people lost their jobs, sending the unemployment rate to 10 percent in October 2009.^([8](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-8)) Arguably, the legacy money and economic system in the United States and the world—because the dollar functions as a world reserve currency—had been corrupted.

Bitcoin’s appeal stems from the fact that it is money without a government, a CEO, or a central bank. No group owns or controls it, and, crucially, no group or person can print more to pay for budget deficits, tax cuts, mismanagement, or wars. The total supply is capped at 21 million Bitcoin. It runs on a decentralized network of computers (the nodes), which validate and record all transactions on an open digital ledger called a blockchain. Think of Bitcoin’s network and blockchain as maintaining a huge public spreadsheet that can be added to only if all nodes (the Byzantine generals), and there are currently about 12,000 of them, agree on every transaction.

Initially appealing to a small set of cryptographers and computer scientists driven by problem-solving, Bitcoin soon attracted an array of technologists, libertarians, entrepreneurs, investors, and gawkers recognizing an innovative alternative to a deeply flawed economic system. The first known Bitcoin transaction was purchase of two pizzas for 10,000 Bitcoin in 2010. That cost would be US$230 million at the recent price of $23,000 per Bitcoin. The current market value for all Bitcoin is about $432 billion. This is roughly one-tenth the current value of the world’s gold supply. However, Bitcoin’s value has been growing faster than gold’s by an order of magnitude over the past decade, as it becomes increasingly mainstream.

Bitcoin is extremely volatile, with price reductions of 80 percent or more on multiple occasions, and it remains a high-risk bet. Many critics view it as a threat to governments, whose power is linked to their control of money supplies. Proponents, however, believe this decentralized monetary innovation has impressive advantages.^([9](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-9)) It can’t be diluted by printing more, unlike all national (fiat) currencies, and it can’t be forged. Bitcoin itself, unlike some centralized exchanges and custodians, has never been hacked. Fees charged by banks or other financial institutions for accessing and sending money (friction) are eliminated or reduced, and the speed of transfer across national and international jurisdictions is measured in seconds or minutes rather than days or weeks. It’s value is increasing over time, unlike fiat currencies, because, as advocates say, it is based on scarcity, which will only increase with increasing demand. People living in countries with local currency crises (e.g., El Salvador, Venezuela, Turkey, Argentina, Brazil) are said to have an option for maintaining their savings, as long as there are no political or social barriers to their participation in the Bitcoin network.

### Life

The terms “centralization” and “decentralization” are most often applied to systems of governance, economics, or technology to describe forms of control.

Their application to life’s evolution is unusual; however, it brings a new conceptual framework and the potential for insight and comparison with other systems where the terms are more common. *Centralization* denotes control restricted to one or a few authorities. In *decentralized* systems, control is shared among many entities. A government where one person makes all important decisions is centralized. A government where local officials make their own decisions is decentralized. Here, I use the concept of decentralization for life’s phylogeny—its evolutionary relationships—to augment the term “network.” In saying life’s phylogeny is networked, and therefore decentralized to some degree, I am emphasizing the sharing of genetic materials outside the confines of parents and their progeny. For example, decentralization for plant evolution, by horizontal gene transfer into plants from nonplants, is shown in [figure 1.3](#08_ch1.xhtml#fig1-3).

To the extent that creatures experience any of the mechanisms of horizontal evolution, whether recently or in the distant past, they embody at least some decentralization of life’s genetic material. To the extent they have not experienced horizontal evolution, their genealogy is more centralized and more hierarchical. These are not binary, all-or-none, conditions for organisms and their genomes. Groups of organisms and species fall along a continuum of more or less centralized. And these may vary among individuals and over time.

Decentralizing horizontal evolution brings a similar potential for innovation among life-forms, as noted above for nonbiological entities, like communications and money (see [box 2.1](#09_ch2.xhtml#box2-1)). Without horizontal evolution, genetic variation within species is limited to that which already exists plus the incremental mutations associated with reproduction and DNA replication. Asexual reproduction is clonal and, in the absence of horizontal evolution, even more restrictive of variation. Trends for centralized and decentralized systems are also compared for human organizations and genetic heritability in [table 2.1](#09_ch2.xhtml#tab2-1).

| Table 2.1. Comparison of trends in characteristics of hierarchical versus networked organizations and biological heritability |
| --- |
| Hierarchies || Networks |
| --- | --- |
| **Human Organizations** |
| Perform better in stable environmentsHarder to change; slower to adaptSecurity easier to maintainUnits: states, businesses, departments || Perform better in changeable environments Easier to change; faster to adaptSecurity harder to maintainUnits: people |
| **Biological Heritability** |
| Genetic information is more stable, less variable across generationsSlower pace of genomic change and evolutionary innovationGenome integrity and function easier to maintainUnits: species, other taxa || Genetic information is less stable, more variable across generationsFaster pace of genomic change and evolutionary innovationGenome integrity and function harder to maintainUnits: genes, functions |

Horizontal evolution accelerates the transmission of DNA and all that it encodes or influences. This includes proteins, immunities, body parts, behaviors, and more. Horizontal evolution events are mutations, and, as is well known, most mutations disrupt the lives of organisms, long honed by natural selection, in ways that are not helpful. But some of these mutations may be advantageous by chance or in changed environments, allowing those mutations to be selected for and to succeed over time. Horizontal evolution also brings potential for genetic variation of greater consequence, compared with vertical, parent-to-progeny change. This includes bringing genes and functions that are more divergent, or simply absent, from those of the recipient species. The horizontal sharing of heritable materials and the traits they influence adds elements of speed and unpredictability to organismal change, beyond the confines of vertical evolution from parents to progeny.

In many ways and for many groups, the living world is more fluid than previously imagined. Ridley notes: “The secret sauce that leads to innovation is freedom. Freedom to exchange, experiment, imagine, invest and fail.”^([10](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-10)) In the context of heritability, the mechanisms of horizontal evolution can be that freedom and secret sauce for innovation.

#### Mutation and Creativity

There is an interesting paradox here. Horizontal evolution can be a secret sauce for innovation. Yet, events of horizontal evolution begin as mutations, which are also known to be errors in copying genetic materials. It’s inauspicious, but it’s also how evolution works. Life’s nearly infinite innovations all begin as mutations. Wings, brains, immune systems, sex, multicellularity, and eusociality—as in bees with sterile workers—all trace their origins to errors in copying and transferring of DNA sequences as they pass from parents to offspring or donors to recipients. Mutations are often neutral or detrimental in their effects on organisms. There are countless ways to make already-functioning genes and organisms worse, and relatively few to make them better. However, without mutations, change by natural selection is hobbled. Life without mutation would be caught in a 3.8-billion-year loop, a bad version of the movie *Groundhog Day*. So, let’s admit, mutation is also good.

Given its link to error, mutation receives much less attention as a creative force in evolution than natural selection. Darwin’s and Wallace’s seminal works in the nineteenth century were preoccupied with making the case for the powers of natural selection, shaping nature’s glory from an ocean of mostly minor variants. But they were unaware of molecular biology or then-invisible genomes and their potential for quick and consequential change. The study of bacteria was only beginning, and an interoperability of heritable materials across very different species could not be foreseen.

One of the oldest continuing controversies in evolutionary biology is whether evolution is primarily driven by selection or mutation. Personally, I think the controversy is more imagined than real. Nearly everyone agrees that both are important and mutually dependent in the flow of evolution. However, selection has often been seen as primary when the mutations being discussed are small-scale mutations, often changes of single nucleotide positions in a string of DNA. But as we’ve learned more about the full range of mutations that happen, and the range of biases in mutation that organisms show, the idea of mutation as creative force in evolution has been expanding. We now know of myriad kinds of DNA duplications, from single nucleotides to parts of genes to whole genomes. There are DNA insertions and losses, as well as mutations in chromosome structure to contend with, along with all the mutations arising from the material horizontal evolution mechanisms (hybridization, microbial recombination, endosymbioses, and horizontal gene transfer) that are the focus of this book. Mutation as mere error is too narrow. Mutations are also unwitting experiments in which DNA sequence alterations might play out as sustaining innovation and adaptivity.

Masatoshi Nei, a pioneer in evolutionary genetics, argued that most functional genes are constrained—they can’t change or they lose their function—and that rare constraint-breaking mutations are the actual drivers of evolution, more so than selection. Further, he suggests the existence of suitable habitats without species, poorly adapted species, and species extinctions argues against the power of natural selection and small mutations (single nucleotide changes within DNA sequences) to accomplish what evolution has wrought.^([11](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-11))

Horizontal evolution, when it persists, tends to be more disruptive than change at single DNA sequence positions. Processes of horizontal evolution operate on a different and larger scale. Hybridization can mix the full set of genes from different species. Joining can add an entire genome, yielding a composite taxon, and horizontal gene transfer can bring full genes and sets of genes that are new to a species. This results in not only the presence and potential of new genes but new gene combinations as well. Species that are only distantly related can share genetic material, and become integrated in this way.

Sets of genes may be gained by horizontal evolution that had been shaped by natural selection in their previous host to work together. This also means innovation can happen faster. Gaining photosynthesis is a bigger and faster change compared with a single nucleotide substitution. Although these features may reduce functional stability of the organisms receiving genetic materials horizontally, they may also provide, by chance, variation that is useful and adaptive, perhaps when the environment is variable. That variability may include unusual weather or new competitors, predators, parasites, and pathogens. Changing knowledge of the scope and pace of mutation to include horizontal evolution events lends it a greater and more creative role in evolution than once thought. Imagine that some of your day-to-day mistakes yield brilliant, unexpected consequences. You go to the wrong airport terminal, miss your flight, and then meet the love of your life while cursing and killing time.

### Four Billion Years of Horizontal Evolution

To appreciate the scope and significance of horizontal evolution across all life’s history, let’s consider horizontal evolution events, following an evolutionary timeline. This includes overviews of horizontal evolution in life’s origins and across the two primary groups of organisms, bacteria and archaea, and their combined offshoot, eukaryotes. I’ll call attention to horizontal evolution’s influence on several innovations deemed to be major transitions in the history of life.^([12](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-12)) These are the transition to multicellularity, use of oxygen and sunlight, and sexual reproduction. My purpose here is to exemplify the support for evolution’s new narrative.

#### Life’s Origins and Early Evolution

Life arose about 3.8 billion years ago following hundreds of millions of years of chemical interactions in which carbon compounds and water were key players. The natural properties of chemicals, together with the constraints of physics, led to self-replicating chemical compounds and the emergence of what would become cell membranes made with lipids, which, importantly, do not dissolve in water. These features enabled the natural evolution of single-celled life ancestral to all species. Although many details and questions remain to be resolved, numerous steps in this process of life’s origins have been observed in both nature and lab experiments.^([13](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-13))

Microbiologist Carl Woese first developed the scenario in which this earliest time in life’s evolution, prior to the divergences of archaea, bacteria, and eukaryotes, was dominated by horizontal gene transfer. He suggested that the ancestor for those primary lineages “was communal, a loosely knit, diverse conglomeration of primitive cells that evolved as a unit.… \[A\]ny lineages evolving barriers to acquisition or expression of foreign genes would be left behind in the evolutionary progression toward modern life. Lateral gene transfer of this kind … would completely dominate the primitive evolutionary dynamic.”^([14](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-14)) He coined the term “Darwinian threshold” for the time when life transitioned from primarily horizontal evolution, with widespread sharing of genes, to greater vertical evolution with more or most genes moving between single-celled parents and their offspring as cell design became more complex and less flexible*.*^([15](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-15)) Although this idea is plausible, there is no evidence to support it, and this is as might be expected. No species with enough permanence to leave a genealogical trace could exist on the early side of any such threshold owing to the ephemeral nature of the “*conglomeration*.” We are left with a reasonable yet untestable supposition, at least for now. The important roles of horizontal evolution and decentralization in life’s origins, along with the involvement of viruses, will be considered in more detail in [chapter 4](#11_ch4.xhtml).

#### Bacteria and Archaea

Life’s earliest divergences gave rise to our two largest groups of single-celled organisms, bacteria and archaea, between 3.5 and 4 billion years ago.^([16](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-16)) These two groups combined represent an estimated 78 percent of Earth’s species today (excluding viruses; see [table 1.3](#08_ch1.xhtml#tab1-3)). They are ubiquitous across Earth’s land and waters, including some found in extreme environments such as hot springs, hydrothermal vents, the Earth’s crust (more than a mile below the ocean floor), and extremes of salinity, desiccation, cold, and radioactivity. They also compose the microbiome residing within individual humans and all other eukaryotes. There are even some bacteria living it up within other bacteria.^([17](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-17)) No doubt about it, they have been wildly successful in diversifying and adapting to Earth’s many opportunities.

Bacteria and archaea are asexual. There are no reproductive cells, like eggs or sperm, and no individuals are deemed to be males or females. But does that mean microbes are barred somehow from the benefits of sex? Must they abstain from the genetic diversity brought when males and females contribute their differences to offspring? Is diversity-limiting clonal reproduction their only option? Of course not. Genetic recombination happens across all life-forms but is particularly frequent in bacteria and archaea (and viruses), and is analogous to sexual reproduction, and potentially hybridization, among eukaryotes. We just don’t call it sex. There are three basic mechanisms for bacterial and archaeal recombination, but their impacts in genetic mixing are similar. They are (1) transformation, importing DNA from the environment; (2) transduction, transferring DNA among bacteria by viruses; and (3) conjugation, transferring DNA by cell-to-cell contact. All forms of recombination increase genetic diversity within those groups.

Recombination by bacteria and archaea is estimated to be nearly as old as the groups themselves, based on estimated ages of relevant genes.^([18](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-18)) This places extensive horizontal evolution in the early stages of life’s evolution. The potential for early microbial life-forms to share, recombine, miscopy, and share again their genetic materials, including suites of genes honed to accomplish particular functions, makes for a powerful engine of innovation. This can result in new species with new capabilities. New genetic material and new combinations of genes may serve organisms well when they find themselves in altered environments, or when new competitors and pathogens threaten their populations. Recombination is also commonplace among close relatives within bacteria and archaea.

Like recombination, horizontal gene transfer has occurred within bacteria and within archaea throughout their evolution. The bacterial species *Mesotoga infera* provides a good example of the extensive horizontal gene transfer and decentralization of genetic materials that can be identified in living individuals. Identification of horizontal gene transfer is done by comparing genomes from *M*. *infera* with representatives of all the major groups of bacteria. Individuals donating gene copies to *M*. *infera* via horizontal gene transfer, over time, span the full diversity of bacteria, as described by the arrows in [figure 2.2](#09_ch2.xhtml#fig2-2).^([19](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-19)) Let that sink in. A rough analogy would be if a human genome were found to have unique genes from diverse eukaryotes, including a selection animals, plants, and fungi.

Those diverse gene origins within *Mesotoga infera* denote decentralization of its genome over time within bacteria. But what is the average extent of genome decentralization across bacterial species? Another recent study estimates that 30 to 50 percent of bacterial genomes on average stem from horizontal gene transfer events.^([20](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-20)) The horizontally transferred genes and the potential innovations they represent are functionally important in the daily lives of their bacterial recipients. They encode genes used in metabolizing food; copying and translating DNA into proteins; and the defense, signaling, and movement of bacterial cells. The transferred genes, and the innovations they entail, matter. Estimates of the amounts of horizontal gene transfer represented within genomes will change as tools improve and more genomes are sequenced. But the existence of meaningful decentralization of genetic materials and the resulting integration among diverse groups of bacteria is clear.

Archaea show similar trends to bacteria in the frequency of horizontal evolution. Comparisons of archaeal genes and genomes across diverse groups show that an average of 12 to 22 percent of genes are the result of past horizontal gene transfer events ([figure 2.3](#09_ch2.xhtml#fig2-3)). As with bacteria, these estimates for archaea span important categories of gene function and innovation, indicating their integral roles in the structure and maintenance of living archaeal individuals.^([21](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-21))

#### Eukaryotes

Next within the sequence of divergence among life’s primary groups is the divergence within archaea that gave rise to eukaryotes about 1.5 billion years ago. Both archaea and eukaryotes have continued a degree of genetic sharing with bacteria from their first divergence up to recent times. Eukaryotes is our group, including an estimated 22 percent of Earth’s species. These are the animals, plants, fungi, protists, and their immediate ancestors.

Diversification within eukaryotes began with small organisms, now long gone, that we would consider to be protists, the name for eukaryotes that are not animals, plants, or fungi. Though still controversial, some experts posit the early diverging protists were “almost certainly” single-celled predators able to swallow individual bacteria or archaea.^([22](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-22)) This ability to ingest organic material as food is a hallmark of eukaryotes, distinguishing them from bacteria and archaea. Three different eukaryotic protists caught in the act of predation are shown in [figure 2.4](#09_ch2.xhtml#fig2-4). Microscopic observations of protist predation are relatively recent, and as the behavior of more protists is scrutinized, predation is turning out to be more common than imagined.

##### MAKING USE OF OXYGEN AND SUNLIGHT

One of the most consequential lineage joining events for eukaryotes was the endosymbiosis (one organism living inside another) giving rise to mitochondria, about 1.8 billion years ago. The lineages that joined were an archaea species ancestral to eukaryotes and a bacteria species from a species-rich group called the Alphaproteobacteria.^([23](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-23)) To set the stage, the amount of oxygen in Earth’s air had been increasing owing to the activities of photosynthesizing bacteria. Most life-forms at the time were anaerobic, and unable to cope with the increasing oxygen levels. Some ancient Alphaproteobacteria, however, evolved the ability to metabolize oxygen, breaking it down and creating a useful energy source (adenosine triphosphate, or ATP). Over time, anaerobic archaea that engulfed and joined with an incipient oxygen metabolizer would be an evolutionary winner, giving rise to eukaryotic cells containing and powered by mitochondria.^([24](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter2-24)) This primary endosymbiosis, yielding mitochondria, has been of fundamental importance to the evolution of nearly all eukaryotic species, helping them diversify and colonize a richly oxygenated Earth.

Both use appendages, some poisonous, to battle each other. B, An amoeba is shown consuming several bacteria. C, A freshwater flagellate containing chloroplasts in genus Rapaza is shown consuming a member of Tetraselmis, a genus of green alga. Drawings are based on photographs in Leander, B.
