This is the ingenious process in which cells use oxygen to make the energy they need to function. This turned out to be more than just a means for survival. Cellular respiration is nearly 20 times more efficient as a means of energy production compared with fermentation as used by anaerobic organisms. This greater efficiency in energy production (as measured in molecules of adenosine-5′\-triphosphate—ATP—output per molecule of glucose) has allowed oxygen-metabolizing organisms to grow larger, develop faster, be more active, and ultimately dominate many of Earth’s habitats.^([5](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter6-5)) A key feature of the evolution of cellular respiration is the symbiotic coevolution of eukaryotic (nucleated) cells having chloroplasts and mitochondria. As noted earlier, these intracellular organelles are descendants of formerly free-living aerobic bacteria and are capable of photosynthesis and oxygen metabolism, respectively. Their presence allows the organisms bearing them to thrive in oxygenated environments. In this way, oxygen stimulated the evolution of multicellular organisms like plants and animals, with their first appearance post-dating the early accumulation of atmospheric oxygen. Finally, oxygen availability is linked to many adaptive innovations in animals requiring its abundance, including warm-bloodedness, permeable eggshells, and live births like your own.

Increasing atmospheric oxygen has significantly altered Earth’s environment as well. It warms Earth’s surface by retaining heat (part of the greenhouse effect) and reducing temperature differences between day and night. Oxygen forms ozone in the presence of ultraviolet (UV) light or lightning, and the ozone layer, 10–50 kilometers above Earth’s surface, filters out more damaging UV sunlight than does oxygen alone. Unabsorbed UV sunlight causes sunburn in humans and can cause DNA damage in living organisms. In these ways, life has facilitated its own evolution.

Every breath you take demonstrates the coevolution of our ancient ancestors with their photosynthesizing bacterial cousins. These bacterial oxygen producers have molded our lifestyle via natural selection and helped to set, and currently maintain, the optimal atmospheric conditions for human health. The descendants of the early oxygen producers, including chloroplasts, are all around us, and yet their diversity and our dependency on them are often overlooked. Some estimate that it would take about 2,000 years to regenerate all the oxygen in the atmosphere at current rates of photosynthesis.

Let’s consider the nature and role of three extant photosynthesizing species that form part of our life-support system: a single-celled marine cyanobacterium, a freshwater alga, and a terrestrial plant. The three oxygen-producing species will be shown to be distinct from one another, yet linked to one another and to their communities through a series of symbiotic relationships.

#### Marine Photosynthesizing Bacteria

*Prochlorococcus marinus* is a tiny photosynthesizing marine bacterium, with a prodigious collective output of oxygen ([figure 6.6](#14_ch6.xhtml#fig6-6)). These cyanobacteria average 0.7 micrometers in width, or one-hundredth the diameter of a human hair, and are smaller than some viruses. Individuals have only 1,800 or so genes, compared with 20,000 for humans. But their broad oceanic distribution and their unique ability to survive in nutrient-poor areas of the ocean render them superabundant. They are estimated to provide more than 50 percent of all photosynthesis in the open ocean and, roughly, they provide the oxygen in one of every five breaths that you take. Their abundance and use of carbon dioxide during photosynthesis make them high-impact players in the global cycling of carbon as well. Nutrient-poor areas of the ocean are currently increasing, sadly, and *Prochlorococcus*’s relative contribution to global oxygen supplies and carbon cycling is very likely to grow over time.

 ![FIGURE 6.6. Prochlorococcus marinus is a tiny photosynthesizing marine cyanobacterium. It is a significant player both in oxygen production and in the global cycling of carbon. Its subsequent importance to other species makes it integral to environmental health.](images/Mindell_Evolu_Fig_6_6.jpg)

FIGURE 6.6. *Prochlorococcus marinus* is a tiny photosynthesizing marine cyanobacterium. It is a significant player both in oxygen production and in the global cycling of carbon. Its subsequent importance to other species makes it integral to environmental health.

Identifying and naming discrete species of bacteria, which reproduce clonally, is more subjective than doing so in sexually reproducing groups like birds or mammals, where reproductive isolation helps in designating species. Currently, a single species of *Prochlorococcus* is identified (*P*. *marinus*); however, there are a growing number of ecotypes (populations uniquely adapted to local environments) being recognized.^([6](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter6-6)) *Prochlorococcus* ecotypes include populations inhabiting ocean layers with various combinations of light (depth) levels and nutrient levels. One of the keys to *Prochlorococcus*’s success is its ability to inhabit places inhospitable to competitors, and mounting evidence suggests that small cell and genome sizes are adaptive advantages for *Prochlorococcus* in nutrient-poor waters. Small genomes can be replicated faster and with less metabolic cost. They can also be packaged in smaller cells, having more surface area per unit volume, which can, in turn, optimize light absorption.

Given our goal of examining a few species that we rely on for oxygen, let’s go one step further and consider how *Prochlorococcus*’s evolution has been helped by some viruses. The genome size of *Prochlorococcus* averages about two million bases (2 Mb) for the 1,800 genes mentioned. This is among the smallest of genome sizes found in cyanobacteria, which range in size up to 9.1 Mb. *Prochlorococcus* and its relatives are considered to approximate the minimum possible size for photosynthetic microbes. One of the perils of evolving small genome size is loss of genes and genetic variation, which are important for survival in different and changing environments. The compact *Prochlorococcus* genome is, however, known to benefit from the horizontal transfer of genes by viruses that infect cyanobacteria, known as cyanophages. This includes horizontal transfer of genes involved in photosynthesis both among *Prochlorococcus* populations and between *Prochlorococcus* populations and some cyanophages, which maintain photosynthesis genes of their own. By providing a mechanism for adding novel genes and gene variants, horizontal gene transfer mediated by cyanophages helps enable adaptive evolution of *Prochlorococcus* forms specializing in survival under changing light and nutrient regimes in the ocean. This horizontal gene transfer via marine viruses adds yet another group of organisms, cyanophages, to the network supporting global oxygen supplies. It’s also another example of the importance and speed of horizontal evolution. Evolution’s new narrative incorporates and highlights these patterns of horizontal connection.

#### Green Algae

*Volvox carteri* is a single-celled, chloroplast-bearing freshwater alga and another impressive oxygen producer ([figure 6.7](#14_ch6.xhtml#fig6-7)). Well known among biologists for its ability to span single and multicellular lifestyles, thousands of *Volvox* individuals can join in forming a translucent colonial sphere, which rolls and twirls to the coordinated beating of each individual’s tail-like flagella. Colonies swim in search of light for photosynthesis, and, occasionally, they dance in pairs and cross-fertilize. *Volvox* colonies include two cell types, numerous somatic cells forming the sphere and a smaller number of reproductive cells contained within the sphere. Given its transitional lifestyle, as an organism capable of both single and multicellular living, *Volvox* has become a model organism for studying the evolution of multicellularity. The molecular and developmental changes involved in this particular transition to multicellularity are relatively few and took place early during the evolution of *Volvox*, about 200 million years ago.^([7](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter6-7))

*V*. *carteri* is one of 22,000 or so species of green algae, which range from single-celled flagellates to large multicellular seaweeds and kelp. Green algae as a group are abundant, providing primary food sources for many fresh- and saltwater organisms, and contributing significantly to the global oxygen supply. This is also the group whose ancestors gave rise to oxygen-producing land plants. The role of lineage merger is pervasive in the oxygenation of Earth’s biosphere, with multiple independent merger or joining events between cyanobacteria and various lineages of green algae, red algae, land plants, and glaucophytes (see [figure 2.5](#09_ch2.xhtml#fig2-5)).

![FIGURE 6.7. Volvox carteri is a single-celled, chloroplast-bearing freshwater green alga and another abundant oxygen producer. Thousands of Volvox individuals can join to form translucent colonial spheres, which move in search of light for photosynthesis. Collectively, green algae are primary food sources for many organisms and contribute significantly to the global oxygen supply. ¶ Photo courtesy of Frank Fox / www.mikro-foto.de, CC-SA 3.0.](images/Mindell_Evolu_Fig_6_7.jpg)

FIGURE 6.7. *Volvox carteri* is a single-celled, chloroplast-bearing freshwater green alga and another abundant oxygen producer. Thousands of *Volvox* individuals can join to form translucent colonial spheres, which move in search of light for photosynthesis. Collectively, green algae are primary food sources for many organisms and contribute significantly to the global oxygen supply. ¶ Photo courtesy of Frank Fox / [www.mikro\-foto.de,](http://www.mikro-foto.de) CC-SA 3.0.

#### Land Plants

Quaking aspen (*Populus tremuloides*) is the most widely distributed native tree species in North America, ranging from Alaska and northeastern Canada south through the Rocky Mountains and into Mexico. Like all photosynthesizers, quaking aspen trees release oxygen as a by-product, separating oxygen from the carbon in carbon dioxide molecules. The amount of oxygen released by trees varies greatly depending on tree species, individual size, and environment, but rough estimates are that the oxygen consumption of one adult human is offset by the activity of roughly 10 to 20 trees.

Quaking aspens are unusual in reproducing both sexually and clonally through root sprouts. Their seeds lack a protective coating and stored nutrients, so they are viable for a relatively short period of time, and their role in reproduction is limited, especially in arid western North America. There is a huge quaking aspen individual, a colony of clones, in Utah with genetically identical trees (or stems) connected by a single root system, which covers 105 acres, weighs 6.6 tons, and is estimated to be at least 80,000 years old. This individual must rank high in terms of the greatest oxygen contribution by a plant.

It is the ability of land plants like the quaking aspen to adapt and colonize Earth’s diverse habitats, from deserts to tropics and tundra, that provided the secondary push in adding oxygen to the atmosphere, following the evolution of photosynthesizing bacteria. The colonization of land began about 475 million years ago, well before oxygen first reached current levels of 20 percent, and has resulted in a proliferation of forms, including an estimated 435,000 extant species of land plants.

### Water

All known life-forms require water, just as we do. The need for water is about more than satisfying thirst or even growing food. Water provides the requisite solvent that allows carbon, nitrogen, and oxygen to react and form structures like DNA and proteins. This, in turn, allows individual organisms to exist and metabolize, as necessary for reproduction and survival. Without water as a chemical solvent, our cells could not produce energy. Life’s need for water is fundamental. No substitute is known.

How did Earth’s water arise? Unlike atmospheric oxygen or soil, Earth’s water is not a by-product of life. Rather, evidence suggests that water, now observed to be prevalent in the universe in gaseous clouds, is a by-product of star formation and was most likely brought to Earth by asteroids. Earthbound processes such as leakage from water-containing minerals in rocks may have contributed as well. The important roles that species play in maintenance of water have to do with purifying water by removing sediments and pollutants, and by helping regulate the flow of water around the world, including mitigating floods. We’ll briefly consider caddisflies and mangrove plants as examples.

#### Aquatic Insect Larvae

The grey flag caddisfly (*Hydropsyche pellucidula*) is one of 15,000 caddisfly species in the exclusively aquatic insect order Trichoptera ([figure 6.8](#14_ch6.xhtml#fig6-8)). The adults look like small, narrow-winged moths with unusually long antennae, and they ingest only liquids during their few weeks or months on land. The aquatic larvae, however, feed on detritus, which they collect by seining freshwater streams with elaborate nets of their own design. They spin the nets from silk produced in their specialized salivary glands, adding sand, pebbles, and twigs as needed. The collected and consumed detritus includes small algae, plant, and animal materials, as well as their many fungal and bacterial decomposers. The relative abundance of *H*. *pellucidula* and other caddisflies is a positive indication of water quality, as the larvae quickly accumulate water contaminants and suffer the effects of them, and of and low oxygen levels. Although the impact of individual caddisflies on water purity is negligible, the cumulative effect of large populations is significant. Caddisfly experts estimate there may be as many as 60,000 species globally, though surveys for them have been limited.

![FIGURE 6.8. The grey flag caddisfly (Hydropsyche pellucidula) is one of 15,000 caddisfly species in the order Trichoptera, which help to purify freshwater streams and lakes. The narrow-winged adults live on land, and the larvae are aquatic, feeding on decaying bacterial, fungal, plant, and animal materials, which they collect in nets spun with their own silk. Presence and diversity of caddisfly species is a useful indicator of water quality. ¶ Dreamstime / © EPhotocorp.](images/Mindell_Evolu_Fig_6_8.jpg)

FIGURE 6.8. The grey flag caddisfly (*Hydropsyche pellucidula*) is one of 15,000 caddisfly species in the order Trichoptera, which help to purify freshwater streams and lakes. The narrow-winged adults live on land, and the larvae are aquatic, feeding on decaying bacterial, fungal, plant, and animal materials, which they collect in nets spun with their own silk. Presence and diversity of caddisfly species is a useful indicator of water quality. ¶ Dreamstime / © EPhotocorp.

#### Mangroves

The red mangrove (*Rhizophora mangle*) is found in tropical estuaries around the world ([figure 6.9](#14_ch6.xhtml#fig6-9)). Like all 110 known species of mangrove trees, the red mangrove is adapted for growth in waterlogged soils having little available oxygen, high salinity, and frequent tides. Few other plants are able to survive in such environments. Its adaptations include a rubberlike coating on its roots to keep salt out, and stilt roots to prop the mangrove trees up above high tide and to help in aeration through specialized pores in the roots and trunk.

 ![FIGURE 6.9. The red mangrove (Rhizophora mangle) is found in tropical estuaries around the world and is adapted for growth in soils with low oxygen, high salinity, and frequent tides. Its root systems help purify water by trapping organic debris and promoting deposition of fine-grained sediment layers, both of which tend to bind heavy metals and other contaminants. ¶ Dreamstime / © Seadam.](images/Mindell_Evolu_Fig_6_9.jpg)

FIGURE 6.9. The red mangrove (*Rhizophora mangle*) is found in tropical estuaries around the world and is adapted for growth in soils with low oxygen, high salinity, and frequent tides. Its root systems help purify water by trapping organic debris and promoting deposition of fine-grained sediment layers, both of which tend to bind heavy metals and other contaminants. ¶ Dreamstime / © Seadam.

Like many other plants, red mangroves remove some water contaminants at leaf surfaces when returning water vapor to the atmosphere. Red mangrove root systems help purify water by slowing freshwater runoff through estuaries, trapping organic debris, and promoting deposition of fine-grained sediment layers. Decomposing organic materials and fine-grained sediments both have a high capacity for binding heavy metals and other contaminants, making mangrove forests effective in protecting nearby water from such toxins. Removal of mangroves, however, can lead to release of those same contaminants.

Red mangroves and their associated soil bacteria can further improve estuary water quality by consuming excess nutrients like nitrogen and phosphorus in runoff from farm fields. Unchecked, excess nutrients can cause rapid growth of marine algae, depleting oxygen supplies in the water and creating coastal dead zones where few organisms can survive.

Mangroves provide vital habitats for many other species, such as sponges, barnacles, and oysters, but especially so for fish. Recent estimates show that mangrove forests make up only 0.12 percent of the world’s total land area, but support 80 percent of the global fish catch during some part of their life cycle, providing food, habitat, or both.

Mangroves also help buffer shorelines against soil erosion due to waves and tides and, potentially, against the devastating effects of storms and tsunamis. Their efficacy in protecting shorelines will vary from place to place, depending on their expanse and the lay of the land. However, there is no doubt that an ample belt of coastal and tidal-zone vegetation can help dissipate the energy of the large hurricane-driven waves that can scour soils, damage property, and threaten human lives. The story of Naluvedapathi, India, a small village on the Bay of Bengal, and its beneficial tree plantings are now well known. On December 4, 2002, over 80,000 saplings of mostly whistling pines (*Casuarina equisetifolia*), a species which is less salt-tolerant but often found in association with mangroves, were planted across a 20-hectare belt lying between the sea and village. Apparently, this was done to invoke help from the rain gods for this drought-stricken area, and to set a Guinness world record at the same time. Just two years later, however, a tsunami battered the coast of this area, devastating neighboring villages but not Naluvedapathi, which was largely protected by the trees.

#### Viruses

Finally, let’s consider the role of one underappreciated group of viruses known as bacteriophages in maintaining healthy oceans ([figure 6.10](#14_ch6.xhtml#fig6-10)). Their name implies they are bacteria eaters. However, it is more accurate to say they are viruses that infect and destroy bacteria. And that is just the beginning of their impact. There are a staggering number of bacteriophages in the world’s oceans, averaging 50 million individuals per teaspoon of surface seawater. An exemplar species is vibriophage VP06, first identified infecting the marine bacterium *Vibrio parahaemolyticus*. Like us, this phage has a double-stranded DNA genome. Unlike us, it injects its DNAs into a host and co-opts the host’s molecular machinery to reproduce. This process of molecular commandeering of the host is facilitated in at least some cases by viral genes previously gained from bacteria by horizontal gene transfer. The virus progeny then rupture the host’s cell wall, spilling the bits and pieces of bacteria into the ocean.

The actions of one individual vibriophage VP06 doing this are imperceptible. But amplified across the 50 million per teaspoon that manage to infect hosts, the actions are consequential. The bacterial bits provide nutrients and dissolved organic matter, which regulate, by supply and demand, the growth of phytoplankton, which are the bacteria, algae, and protists that make up the basic components of the food web supporting all marine organisms. As part of this process, carbon is recycled into the new organisms and nitrogen is recycled for use by the phytoplankton and those that eat them.^([8](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter6-8))

 ![FIGURE 6.10. Vibriophage VP06 is a bacteriophage. Bacteriophages are viruses that infect and destroy bacteria, and in doing so play an underappreciated role in maintaining healthy oceans. The destroyed bacterial pieces provide nutrients and dissolved organic matter, which regulate the growth of bacteria, algae, and protists, basic components of the food web, helping to support all marine organisms. As part of this process, both carbon and nitrogen are recycled. ¶ Image is from Wong, H. C., Wang, T. Y., Yang, C. W., Tang, C. T., Ying, C., Wang, C. H., and Chang, W. H., 2019, “Characterization of a Lytic Vibriophage VP06 of Vibrio parahaemolyticus,” Research in Microbiology, 170(1):13–23.](images/Mindell_Evolu_Fig_6_10.jpg)

FIGURE 6.10. Vibriophage VP06 is a bacteriophage. Bacteriophages are viruses that infect and destroy bacteria, and in doing so play an underappreciated role in maintaining healthy oceans. The destroyed bacterial pieces provide nutrients and dissolved organic matter, which regulate the growth of bacteria, algae, and protists, basic components of the food web, helping to support all marine organisms. As part of this process, both carbon and nitrogen are recycled. ¶ Image is from Wong, H. C., Wang, T. Y., Yang, C. W., Tang, C. T., Ying, C., Wang, C. H., and Chang, W. H., 2019, “Characterization of a Lytic Vibriophage VP06 of *Vibrio parahaemolyticus*,” *Research in Microbiology*, 170(1):13–23.

Zooming out even further, bacteriophages collectively stimulate the removal of carbon dioxide from the atmosphere and the sequestering of carbon in the ocean floor. They do this by promoting growth in numbers of photosynthetic organisms, which convert carbon dioxide to oxygen and sugars, and the subsequent demise of those organisms (or their predators), whose carbon-rich remains settle into seafloor sediments. Increasing the removal of atmospheric carbon dioxide, one of the primary greenhouse gases, is an important remediation for global climate change. Bacteriophages account for the removal of about three billion tons of carbon annually.

* * *

All the representative species relationships described above illustrate how biological diversity contributes to the origins or maintenance of soil, air, and water. And they beg a larger question. Why do these vital connections between humans and other species exist? Why do we need fertile soils produced by legions of worms, fungi, and bacteria to grow our food? Why do we need oxygen made by *Prochlorococcus marinus* the marine cyanobacterium, *Volvox carteri* the kinetic green alga, and *Populus tremuloides* the quaking aspen? Why do we need water that has been cleared of organic debris and heavy metals by the filtering activities of billions of invertebrate detritivores and plants? Simply put, we need these organisms now because we always have in the past. Our ancestors and theirs coevolved, adapting to shared environments as they found and altered them. This is a pragmatic answer to a pragmatic question. Of course, evolution might have proceeded differently, but it didn’t, and our concern is explaining the vital connections among life-forms that we actually observe.

We have arrived in the present, as sentient creatures dependent on oxygen, water, and soil, owing to evolutionary processes that are and have always been profoundly integrated among life-forms. This integration exemplifies evolution’s new narrative, from the sharing of genes to the mutual shaping of environments and imposing of natural selection’s forces via coevolution. The scope of evolutionary time, spanning billions of years, deepens the integration to the point that it becomes an essential, defining component. There is nothing about human form and function that did not arise from the crucible of life’s integration. Our abilities, our vulnerabilities, our potential for change, and our suitability for life on Earth are all rooted in this history.

Often, we are in denial, or more kindly oblivious, and it shows. Other times we appear unable to act in accordance with our understanding of our own best long-term interests. This leads to the topic of the next chapter. What can we surmise of life’s future, and can we glean any insight from evolution’s new narrative?

## 7 Life’s Outlook

> To think ourselves capable of causing the end of nature is an act of great hubris, for it means forgetting the wildness that dwells everywhere within and around us.
> 
> —WILLIAM CRONON, “THE TROUBLE WITH WILDERNESS; OR, GETTING BACK TO THE WRONG NATURE”

How will humans respond to the impending changes in climate and biodiversity, and what impact will those responses have? Can we minimize environmental damage while still seeking economic growth? Which species will perish and which will thrive? Will today’s tropics, currently with about 78 percent of animal and plant species diversity, continue to hold the most diversity? Or will the tropics become too hot and humid for most species, as some suggest? Some predictions in answer to these questions are more likely than others, but much of life’s future, whether 300 or 3,000 years from now, is unknowable.

I will go out on a very short limb, however, to say that barring an apocalyptic celestial event, life will survive. Bacteria and archaea include remarkably resilient forms, able to survive extremes of temperature, desiccation, toxicity, and even radiation. They can also adapt very quickly, given their short generation times. Human survival seems less certain, given our narrower physical tolerances. Our technology certainly gives us an edge in the survival sweepstakes, but we’ll need to mind technology’s applications with care, as the edge cuts both ways, and technology has its own perils. Still, it would be a mistake to bet heavily against humans and our unique capacity for learning, planning, and survival. Especially given our rise over the past 50,000 years from primates lacking complex language to primates deciphering life’s shared evolution and sending data-gathering probes to the stars.

Regardless of which species survive, life will continue to evolve with or without humans. Natural selection and chance events will still result in differential reproductive success for organisms. And the mechanisms of horizontal evolution will continue as a vital component of life’s story, sharing genetic variation more broadly, if less frequently, across groups of organisms than that passed vertically between parents and progeny. In this chapter I will discuss the current conservation status for vertebrates and a few other groups with sufficient data, examples of species adapting to human-induced environmental change, and the field of synthetic biology as humans design new life-forms or reengineer existing ones.

### Status of Vertebrates

Estimating current status and changes in abundance of species is difficult. Even when focusing on species large enough to see, data on species population sizes are a static and limited sampling of reality. An ambitious international effort among scientists and other experts known as IPBES (Intergovernmental Science-Policy Platform on Biodiversity and Ecosystem Services) was established in 2010 to synthesize information on the status of Earth’s biological diversity to better inform the conservation efforts of governments. This effort is similar in scope to that of the IPCC (Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change) established in 1988, which synthesizes data and an outlook on Earth’s changing climate.

The most recent IPBES report finds that 77 percent of ice-free land and 87 percent of ocean areas have been modified by the direct effects of human activities. These activities include agriculture, natural resource extraction, and human infrastructure construction to meet the needs and desires of our societies. The IPBES estimates that over the past 150 years these modifications brought losses of 83 percent of the mass (by weight) of wild mammals and about 41 percent of the mass of wild plants on the planet. At this point, we and our domesticated animals make up an astounding 96 percent of the total mammalian mass on Earth. Correspondingly, species extinction rates have been much higher over the past few hundred years than prior to large-scale transformation of Earth by humans. The IUCN (International Union for Conservation of Nature) estimates that about 900 vertebrate species have gone extinct in the past 500 years, mostly amphibians, mammals, and fish. It also estimates that 41 percent of amphibian species, 26 percent of mammals, and 14 percent of birds are currently threatened with extinction. We don’t have the basic knowledge needed to make similar estimates for the vast majority of living species.^([1](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-1))

A recent summary of 239 time-series biodiversity studies from across the globe tracked change over time in two key measures: the total number of species present and the composition of local species assemblages.^([2](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-2)) These measures were recorded as consistently as possible at multiple time points. Many of the studies had species inventory data beginning in the late 1960s, and the number of years sampled range up to 10 or more years for the same locations. These data are difficult to collect and to compare because of their many differences in timing and methods. It is, however, the largest compilation of time-series studies that I know of to date. Surprising to some, the findings do not indicate widespread loss of species across the past 60 years, despite some change at specific locations. Predictions of species losses in the tropics in particular were not borne out. More obvious than any loss or gain in the number of species present was the change in the composition of local species assemblages, which varied across regions. Rapid compositional change in marine habitats was nearly double that found in terrestrial habitats. Ongoing monitoring and determination of the reasons for the change in local species composition are important next steps.

We can gain some insight regarding future extinctions, at least in the near term, by looking at the numbers of species considered to be on the brink of extinction because of small population size. Recent analyses indicate that of 29,374 terrestrial vertebrate species with available population size estimates from the IUCN, 515 (1.75%) have fewer than 1,000 individuals ([table 7.1](#15_ch7.xhtml#tab7-1)) and are vulnerable to extinction. Among those 515, 47 percent are continental and 53 percent are on islands. Most are from South America (30%), followed by Oceania (21%), Asia (21%), Africa (16%), North and Central America (11%), and Europe (1%). Further, for 77 of the bird and mammal species on the brink, about 94 percent of their populations have been lost since 1900. At some point, species abundances may become so small that they no longer provide their prior function in supporting ecosystems, existing as “ecological zombies.”^([3](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-3))

 

| Table 7.1. Species vulnerable to extinction |
| --- |
| Class || No. of Species with <1,000 Individuals || No. of Species Evaluated || Percentage of Species at Risk |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Mammals || 74 || 5,459 || 1.36 |
| Birds || 335 || 10,423 || 3.21 |
| Reptiles || 41 || 6,861 || 0.60 |
| Amphibians || 65 || 6,631 || 0.98 |
| Total || 515 || 29,374 || 1.75 |
| *Source*: Ceballos, G., Ehrlich, P. R., and Raven, P. H., 2020, “Vertebrates on the Brink as Indicators of Biological Annihilation and the Sixth Mass Extinction,” *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the USA*, 117(24):13596–602. |
| Species were evaluated by the International Union for Conservation of Nature to determine their conservation status. |

We are dramatically changing Earth’s environment and the living conditions for its surviving inhabitants, including ourselves. No doubt about it. The figures above focus on vertebrates because we have even less understanding of species diversity and trends among other life-forms. Our environmental impacts are an unwitting experiment that bodes ill for many other species, and for our notions of a sustainably healthy planet. Despite over a century of deliberation by policymakers and conservation groups, the primary threats to biodiversity of habitat loss and climate change continue, as an expanding human population of nearly eight billion strives for both survival and higher living standards. We should prioritize conservation efforts and save what we can before the opportunity is lost.

But we should also admit we don’t know how the situation will play out. Our strong emotional attachments to the natural world, including favored species and landscapes, cast their losses as personal and deep. Feelings of a looming existential threat come next, and we set ourselves up for hopelessness or a battle against change that we can only lose. We understand that extinction waves have happened in the past, that species and ecosystems are inherently changeable, and that over long time spans extinction is the rule for species—and survival the exception. But that is little consolation. This time we bear responsibility. The cause is not an external, uncontrollable event such as a meteor collision or climate change for reasons unrelated to humans. Current losses are rapid and due to the activities of a single technology-driven species—that is, us. This compels us to do all we can to mitigate and minimize the losses, whether our reasoning is driven by ethics, aesthetics, self-interest, or their combination.

We are glimpsing large-scale forces at work that will test our own abilities to respond and adapt. We understand that the species that do survive are either lucky or readily adaptable to changing environments. Because we have no guarantees of our own survival, we will need to marshal our best efforts scientifically, socially, and politically to anticipate and respond to the coming changes.^([4](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-4)) Our efforts in conservation, if enhanced and fortunate, can have a positive lasting effect. We don’t know how other species and how ecosystems will respond over time to the ongoing environmental changes, but evolution is a tale of resilience.

### Species Adapting

In our current era, known as the Anthropocene, humans have become the dominant influence on Earth’s environment. Frequent horizontal evolution among organisms, as emphasized by the new narrative for evolution, can be expected in the Anthropocene alongside the other changes. The same human activities causing climate change and biodiversity loss are forcing some species that were separated geographically from one another to live together. The new proximity stems from humans transporting species, intentionally or not, and from human-caused habitat changes driving species movements. This new physical proximity provides more opportunities for the mechanisms of horizontal evolution: hybridization, recombination, horizontal gene transfer, and coevolution, depending on the species.

For example, Asian sika deer imported to Scotland from Japan in 1893 have hybridized with the Scottish red deer, even though these two species diverged from a common ancestor about six million years earlier. Similar introductions of other vertebrates, often for hunting or fishing, leading to hybridization have occurred for species of impala, antelope, gazelles, pheasants, partridges, freshwater whitefish, perch, trout, tilapia, salmon, and cichlids. Habitat changes, rather than introductions, for fish species that have led to hybridization with related species include various perch, shad, suckers, loaches, and silversides, among many others.^([5](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-5))

Hybridization brings the potential for rapid change, incorporating novel genetic materials into organisms and species whose environments and geographic ranges are changing. The novel genetic materials include genes and sets of genes already honed by natural selection in other organisms, and they often have larger, more immediate effects than the single DNA base mutation differences between parents and progeny in vertical evolution.

Hybridization among plants is widespread and particularly well known in Britain due to longstanding interest in gardening and natural history there. In a fascinating series of examples, conservation biologist Chris Thomas describes how hybridization among plants is known to have given rise to seven or eight new species since 1700. This may seem a small number, but this rate of new plant species origins, even without those that may have been missed, is about 100 times faster than was usual in their evolutionary past.^([6](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-6))

This includes at least three new species of ragworts, in the daisy family, arising from the yellow-flowered yorkwort first brought to Britain from southern Italy in the early 1700s. Once in Britain, it spread and hybridized with several preexisting ragwort species. The new hybrids thrived in disturbed habitats such as building sites and the edges of roads and railways. The hybrids evolved differences preventing them from interbreeding with their parental species. Marsh-dwelling cord grasses provide another example, with distinct North American and British species hybridizing in Britain. The resulting *Sporobolus anglicus* spread along the coast of Britain before spreading and being introduced to other estuaries in North America, Asia, and Australia.

The evolution and success of hybrids can create problems for existing species. For example, *S*. *anglicus,* once viewed as beneficial in controlling erosion, has driven out some local marsh-dwelling species as it spread globally. Similarly, on two different occasions, different species of monkey flower, one from North America and one from South America, escaped from British gardens and gave rise to genetically distinct hybrids colonizing stream-side habitats. Hybrid origins from garden species are relatively easy to identify; however, hybrid origins are not limited to garden species. Mapping efforts in Britain have identified over 900 different crosses between plant species living in the countryside.^([7](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-7))

New hybrids may outcompete existing species in some instances, raising difficult questions for conservationists. Should imperiled species be protected from overwhelming hybridization? How and for how long? Evolution is dynamic and we now know that hybridization is a natural part of the evolution of many or most vertebrates. We ourselves have genetic remnants of hybridization with other hominid species, including Neanderthals and Denisovans. From a biological point of view, there is no such thing as genetic purity for species. Species and populations are always changing genetically over time owing to mutation, chance, and selection. Enforcing barriers to hybridization in the long term makes little sense and would be hard or impossible to sustain. Sadly, many species are being and will be lost. However, the Anthropocene, characterized by environmental change and hybridization among species, “could be the fastest acceleration of evolutionary diversification in the last half-billion years,” in the words of Thomas.^([8](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-8)) This is conjecture and intentionally provocative, I suppose, but it does call attention to the fact that environmental change can cut both ways, yielding decreases as well as increases in biological diversity, with more current increases than many expected. More knowledge for more species and regions is needed to identify long-term trends.

* * *

Let’s turn to examples of microbial pathogens adapting to human impacts. We can expect that ongoing climate change will increase exposure for many people to pathogens, and that pathogens’ adaptive responses will include horizontal evolution by means of recombination and horizontal gene transfer. Some nonhuman viruses will have more opportunities to infect humans as encounters between their animal hosts and humans increase. Viruses from bats are particularly likely to have more human encounters. Many bat species are already shifting their ranges in response to climate change, and their long range dispersal capacity, unique among mammals, will bring them into new contact with human populations.^([9](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-9)) There will also be greater selection pressure on some viruses to expand their host range to humans when their regular host populations decrease in size and range.

Increasing urbanization in many parts of the world favors spread of the mosquitoes that transmit viral diseases such as Zika, dengue, and yellow fever. A variety of intestinal diseases can spread quickly when urbanization overwhelms control measures to separate sewage from water supplies. Warming temperatures can and will alter the range of vector species that carry pathogens, such as snails, mosquitoes, and other insects. Deforestation in Asia has been implicated in increasing contact between humans and bats, and contributing to the spread of viruses such as Nipah, Hendra, Ebola, and SARS-CoV-1 (first reported in 2003). Deforestation has also been linked to the expansion of leishmaniasis, a disease caused by single-celled eukaryotes called trypanosomes and spread by biting sand flies.^([10](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-10))

The COVID-19 pandemic has been facilitated by urbanization and frequent travel in spreading the airborne SARS-CoV-2 virus among humans. Since the initial detection of SARS-CoV-2 in late 2019, hundreds of thousands of different lineages have been tracked based on sequencing their RNA genomes, as they adapt and disperse across the globe with human help. SARS-CoV-2 is airborne, and its spread has been faster and as far-reaching as any known pathogen. This capacity and our ability to track it stems from its rapid evolution. This fast change involves frequent horizontal evolution in the form of recombination and horizontal gene transfer. These processes mix genes from different SARS-CoV-2 lineages and potentially from other coronaviruses too. SARS-CoV-2 lineages with novel gene combinations have potential to seed the emergence of distinctive new SARS-CoV-2 variants, to alter the host species and tissue types they can infect, to change their infection success rate, and, as we have are painfully aware, to enhance their ability to elude vaccines.

The SARS-CoV-2 spike protein enables binding to receptors on the host’s cell surfaces, and is key in determining which host species the virus can infect and the virus’s potential transition from mild to highly pathogenic. Researchers have found that the region of the SARS-CoV-2 genome that encodes the spike protein is particularly susceptible to recombination events, explaining some of its rapid evolutionary change as the virus population surges and spreads. This shows the importance of understanding SARS-CoV-2 recombination to recognizing its potential evolution for design of future vaccines.

The origin of SARS-CoV-2 is uncertain and of course controversial. One possibility is that the virus moved into humans from a different species of animal host, aided by recombination events. This scenario is known as a zoonotic origin. Another possibility is that of a lab origin, often called the lab leak hypothesis. In this scenario the virus escaped from a coronavirus research lab in Wuhan, China. Both hypotheses remain viable. Regardless of which scenario is correct, horizontal evolution has played a key role in SARS-CoV-2’s early evolution and rapid spread in humans.^([11](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-11))

Similar connections between horizontal evolution and rapid spread can be seen in other pathogens. The emergence of HIV (human immunodeficiency virus) and AIDS has been linked to recombination events involving SIVcpz (simian immunodeficiency virus in chimpanzees). Following its emergence, HIV recombination has been reported to be as frequent as the single nucleotide changes (point substitutions) characterizing vertical evolution.^([12](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-12))

Influenza viruses cause the flu in a variety of birds and mammals, including people. The symptoms are usually mild, but severe, global-scale pandemics have been occurring in humans every 10–50 years since the late 1800s. The most deadly pandemic killed about 50 million people during 1918–20, before vaccines were available. These flu pandemics have all been caused by novel influenza virus variants, resulting from recombination, a form of horizontal evolution known as antigenic shift or reassortment. This can happen when two or more variants infect the same host cell and swap genes. These influenza recombinants are facilitated when viruses jump from one host species to another. Humans are most at risk from emergent influenza strains from poultry and pig farms. Human infection from influenza circulating in wild birds is rare but possible. If a new influenza recombinant from farms or elsewhere were readily transmissible among humans, dense human populations lacking immunity would be at risk. To bring this into the context of future evolution, warming temperatures can alter the timing and patterns of bird migration, changing species assemblies and contact rates. This, in turn, changes opportunities for transport and reassortment of influenza among bird hosts.^([13](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-13))

Malaria provides a last example of pathogen species adapting via horizontal evolution. Genetic recombination among malaria-causing parasites in the genus *Plasmodium* is known to contribute to their evolutionary success in responding to climate change, making malaria such a scourge. Malaria is estimated to have killed 627,000 people in 2020, most of them children in Africa.^([14](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-14)) The future evolution of human pathogens promises to be fast and episodically furious. Sustained vigilance to minimize outbreaks and proactive control measures will always be essential.

### Conservation of Species and Assisted Evolution

Another component of life’s future evolution will stem from our direct efforts in managing ecosystems and species in the face of environmental change. We pursue many strategies, from legal protections for species and parks, breeding and reintroduction programs, returning species to portions of their former range, and freezing plant seeds for potential future use. The most comprehensive approach is large-scale protection of ecosystems and habitats because of the broad scale and inclusion of diverse species.

Throughout the book I have been highlighting the powers of horizontal evolution in decentralizing the inheritance of genetic material and accelerating change. Will the new narrative for life’s evolution play out in our future efforts to conserve ecosystems and species? Can horizontal evolution be applied as the environments for living systems change? I think so.

Coral reefs are some of the most biologically diverse ecosystems on Earth, providing a home for more than 25 percent of all marine species, including algae, seaweeds, seagrasses, fish, crustaceans, mollusks, worms, sea anemones, jellyfish, tunicates, sponges, and legions of microbes. Coral reefs are named for the colonies of coral invertebrate animals, whose hard skeletons form the reefs. They provide food for more than a billion people, protect thousands of miles of shoreline from erosion by the ocean, and are sources for drugs against cancer, heart disease, and HIV.

Coral reefs also happen to be at risk owing to rising ocean temperatures and changing water chemistry. These factors can be fatal to their small, single-celled endosymbionts known as dinoflagellates, on which corals depend for photosynthesis and food. It is worth noting that these dinoflagellate endosymbionts residing within coral hosts were themselves colonized by photosynthesizing endosymbionts earlier in time, multiplying the impacts of horizontal evolution. Roughly half of the world’s coral reefs have been lost in the past 70 years. If one had to choose a single ecosystem in need of emergency care, coral reefs would be a good candidate.

Researchers have initiated efforts to help coral reefs cope, with an approach known as assisted evolution ([figure 7.1](#15_ch7.xhtml#fig7-1)). One of the components is to simulate horizontal evolution by replacement of corals’ endosymbiont species. This might be accomplished by inoculating early-growth-stage corals with replacement endosymbiont species that are either naturally or genetically engineered to be more stress tolerant. Other components of assisted evolution involve selecting for advantageous changes in gene regulation or stress tolerance in endosymbionts and hosts. Another component of assisted horizontal evolution is to hybridize different species of corals in the lab and monitor their success in stressed environments simulating future conditions of temperature and water chemistry. A natural hybrid coral in the Caribbean has shown slightly greater success in breeding compared with its parental species, indicating the potential of the approach. Other components of assisted evolution involve experimental breeding selection without endosymbiont host switching or hybridization.^([15](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-15))

Replacement of microbial endosymbionts has public health applications in addition to those for conservation in corals outlined above. Consider bacteria in the genus *Wolbachia*. They are common endosymbionts for insects; however, they are not found naturally in *Aedes aegypti*, the mosquito that carries and transmits viruses causing dengue fever, yellow fever, and chikungunya disease in humans. When *A*. *aegypti* is infected with *W*. *pipientis* in the lab, the ability of the mosquito to transmit the viruses is significantly reduced. After eight years of *W*. *pipientis* infection for *A*. *aegypti*, an area around Cairns, Australia, has seen a 96 percent reduction in dengue fever cases. After two years of *W*. *pipientis* infection, an area around Yogyakarta, Indonesia, experienced a reduction in dengue of about 77 percent, and in Niteroi, Brazil, reductions of disease incidence were 69 percent for dengue fever and 56 percent for chikungunya, despite the infection rate for the mosquito with *Wolbachia* varying between 40 and 80 percent over time.^([16](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-16)) In these examples, applying knowledge of the ability for different species to integrate themselves benefits human populations and public health.

![FIGURE 7.1. Summary of the rationale and steps involved in four assisted evolution approaches proposed for the conservation of corals. ¶ Drawing modified from Van Oppen, M. J., Oliver, J. K., Putnam, H. M., and Gates, R. D., 2015, “Building Coral Reef Resilience through Assisted Evolution,” Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the USA, 112(8):2307–13.](images/Mindell_Evolu_Fig_7_1.png)

FIGURE 7.1. Summary of the rationale and steps involved in four assisted evolution approaches proposed for the conservation of corals. ¶ Drawing modified from Van Oppen, M. J., Oliver, J. K., Putnam, H. M., and Gates, R. D., 2015, “Building Coral Reef Resilience through Assisted Evolution,” *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the USA*, 112(8):2307–13.

Another approach that mimics horizontal evolution, known as genetic rescue, can potentially save species or populations at risk owing to low genetic diversity and excessive inbreeding. Genetic rescue seeks to increase genetic diversity and population growth by introducing new individuals or new populations with genetic variation that complements what remains in the populations at risk. There are many examples of species that have already benefitted from genetic rescue, including Florida panthers, black-footed ferrets, arctic foxes, gray wolves, various shrimp, oysters, ryegrasses, and pitcher plants, among others.^([17](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-17))

The use of a genome-editing tool known as CRISPR-Cas9, co-opted from bacteria, also holds promise. This editing tool might help conserve species and potentially ecosystems needing rapid evolutionary change for species to match rapid environmental change.^([18](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-18)) With this approach, genes can be inserted, deleted, replaced, or modified, targeting specific functions while retaining desirable traits, for any organism. This is similar to horizontal gene transfer, but in human hands. Successful editing or recoding of genomes in only one or a few individuals, however, would not spread quickly in sexually reproducing species. This is because progeny inherit half their genes from each parent. The spread of genome edits can be accelerated, however, using a technique known as a gene drives.

Gene drives increase the probability, potentially up to 100 percent, that a particular gene or set of genes will be inherited and spread. This is accomplished by combining the new genetic edits with the CRISPR-Cas9 genes themselves. As a result, the driven genes and the editing mechanism’s genes are all inserted. The insertion site is key in forcing the cell’s repair system to add the editing mechanism to all the organism’s reproductive cells (sperm or eggs).^([19](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-19))

Gene drives are in active development and have not yet been widely deployed. They raise questions of safety and ethics that require careful consideration. Some potential applications are for accelerating lab research only. Others are for species or populations in nature, bringing new opportunities for conservation and management of crops and disease. Gene drives could be used to disrupt reproduction of invasive pest species, including predators such as rats and snakes that have been inadvertently introduced to islands by humans. In an infamous case, cane toads from South and Central America (*Rhinella marina*) were introduced to Australia in 1935 to control beetles that were attacking sugar cane crops. But exotic cane toad populations grew quickly and expanded their range. The toads naturally produce toxins that inadvertently kill many predatory birds and reptiles seeking a meal, including several species of large monitor lizards and the Australian freshwater crocodile. Implementation of gene drives to prevent the cane toads from producing toxins could help the Australian native species survive. This would be safer than the use of poisons, which would impact species beyond the cane toad.

Other applications of gene drives could defeat mosquitoes that spread the malaria-causing parasite (*Plasmodium falciparum*) as well as the Zika and dengue fever viruses. Some say application of gene drives to just 1 percent of a wild mosquito population could eliminate their associated malaria within a year.^([20](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-20)) Engineering future disease resistance appears possible too, as genes may be inserted that disrupt virus or bacterial pathogens. Genome editing, or recoding, is already being used in a variety of domestic plants and animals to enhance desired traits.^([21](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-21))

Another set of efforts combining conservation and potential use of CRISPR-Cas9 genome editing is de-extinction.^([22](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-22)) The hope is to restore extinct species (or slightly modified versions) to natural areas, along with some of their functional roles within ecosystems. Candidates for de-extinction include the woolly mammoth and passenger pigeon. Genetic work typically begins with attempted recovery of their genomes from preserved tissues. Preliminary work indicates the technical challenges are daunting. Many argue funds are better spent conserving nonextinct species. However, funding is not a zero-sum game, and audacious efforts to bring back select species may capture minds and generate their own funds.

Some of these approaches are controversial interventions, and caveats are in order for all the ideas sketched above. There is great deal of work to be done before the various methods discussed can be safely implemented. The risks, ethics, and politics of such interventions require careful consideration. The potential upside, however, includes retention or restoration of essential ecosystems and their species, as well as reducing human disease and suffering.

### Synthetic Life

Life created by humans using molecules, chemicals, and a dizzying array of technologies and organismal templates is known as synthetic life. Once restricted to the realms of mythology and science fiction, synthetic life has arrived. It has excited imaginations and been discussed in the news; however, there has been little fanfare and no existential threat or panic. Rather, there has been a flourishing of research aimed at bringing out the potential of synthetic microbial creatures in helping us to manufacture drugs, design new foods, create biosensors to detect infections and cancer, produce energy, detoxify polluted land and water, help sustain ecosystems (as discussed for corals above), or test ideas about natural life’s origins.^([23](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-23))

Synthetic life is a component of synthetic biology, the study and practice of creating novel biological systems of all kinds. Synthetic biology includes the development of gene drives and replacement of endosymbiont species as discussed above, and more. The biological systems to be created, or remodeled, by synthetic biology range from individual genes and genomes to cells and whole organisms. It brings together molecular biology, biotechnology, chemical engineering, computer engineering, and evolutionary science.

In the next 100 years, humans will seed existing biological diversity with new forms targeted for specific applications. How will those organisms fit with the evolutionary narrative for all life? How can we relate synthetic life-forms to life’s genealogy? Synthetic life will be similar to domesticated crop and animal species in that it will experience selection by humans for desired traits. But it will differ in its origins. Domesticated species are descendants of wild progenitors. Chickens are descended from wild red jungle fowl and cows from extinct aurochs. Synthetic life, in contrast, will be a mix of assembly from synthesized components and the edited parts or wholes of one or more progenitors. Synthetic life’s origins are more an integration and networking among life-forms, whereas domestic species (without hybridization) are more a continuation of single lineages.

The significance of this difference in origins is that synthetic life-forms are and will be more malleable, more quickly. The precise control of components, whatever their source, will also allow unprecedented levels of customization. As we have seen, horizontal evolution successfully combining disparate features can yield dramatic change more quickly than conventional evolution based on selection of differences between parents and progeny. In many ways synthetic life is also horizontal evolution in human hands. The decentralization of inheritance that is characteristic of horizontal evolution is in force, but subject to human choice.

The first synthetic bacterium was created in 2010 when the genome of a bacterium infecting cows, *Mycoplasma mycoides*, was synthesized by a machine and transferred into the emptied cell of a related bacterial species, which then successfully reproduced itself. This effort was extended in 2016 using a reduced set of 473 genes for the new life-form, known as *Mycoplasma laboratorium* or Synthia.^([24](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-24)) The first complete synthetic genome for a eukaryote, a modified baker’s yeast, *Saccharomyces cerevisiae*, was generated in 2017 by an international team.^([25](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-25)) In 2019, researchers created a full synthetic genome and variant of the bacterium *Escherichia coli* using a revised genetic code. The revised genetic code enables use of amino acids not encoded by natural organisms (noncanonical amino acids) for a greater variety of protein products.^([26](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-26)) CRISPR-Cas9, as outlined above, edits and recodes existing genomes rather than synthesizing entire genomes de novo. Use of CRISPR-Cas9 began in 2012, and its ongoing development and efficiency have made it the method of choice for many synthetic biology projects, dramatically accelerating progress and applications. Regardless of methods used, the technological challenges and safety issues remain fraught and arduous.

In agriculture, synthetic biology has potential to increase the nutrient density of food crops and their availability to burgeoning human populations. Development of trees with synthetic genomes could enhance rates of carbon sequestration, helping mitigate carbon dioxide emissions and climate change. Synthetic microbes are already being developed to aid in diagnosing and treating diseases such irritable bowel syndrome, to disable pathogens, and to produce novel proteins with novel functions.^([27](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-27)) There are naturally electroactive bacteria that communicate and cooperate by moving electrons in and out of their cells. This ability has been tapped using genome editing to make a novel synthetic electroactive form of *E*. *coli*, in initial efforts to produce sustainable energy.^([28](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-28)) Yet another set of methods, called directed endosymbiosis, could synthesize new kinds of organisms with novel, synthetic organelles. This approach would roughly mimic the origins of chloroplasts and mitochondria in eukaryotes. Synthetic organelles could be used to replace defective mitochondria in humans suffering with a variety of mitochondrial disorders, or to improve efficiency of biological processes such as nitrogen fixation in plants ([figure 7.2](#15_ch7.xhtml#fig7-2)). Synthetic biology and synthetic life are already giving rise to multibillion dollar industries working to make drugs, chemicals, living sensors, fertilizers, textiles, food, and power. No one is discussing it, but one can dream about personalized chloroplasts helping you feed on sunlight.

With synthetic biology, we access powers previously considered superhuman. We can approximate, customize, and accelerate some of horizontal evolution’s most potent mechanisms for fast and radical change. We can emulate the mechanisms of horizontal evolution that are the focus of evolution’s new narrative, and amplify their impact on life’s future. We can choose among features we find in life’s diverse forms and try them in combination, even though our understanding is incomplete regarding how such complex living systems work. That accentuates the need for caution. Further success, even if slow and restricted, seems inevitable. Human imagination has room to run.

* * *

Extending this consideration of future evolution, what if some individuals, human or otherwise, leave Earth and take up residence elsewhere? Is ongoing evolution, both vertical and horizontal, expected? Yes. Why not? Life needs only three features to evolve by natural selection: heredity, variation among individuals, and differential reproductive success tied to that variation. This was Darwin’s basic insight. It holds for all of Earth’s environments and there is no reason this truism should not apply beyond our planet, even if humans were to become arbiters of selection.

 ![FIGURE 7.2. Potential applications of synthetic mitochondria and chloroplasts (organelles) in research. More efficient photosynthesis and cellular respiration in organisms might be developed using synthesized or improved chloroplasts and mitochondria. Synthetic mitochondria could be used in treatment of human diseases linked to mitochondrial problems. Synthetic organelles could also be used to study biological processes requiring compartmentalization, such as nitrogen fixation. ¶ Diagram modified from Meaney, R. S., Hamadache, S., Soltysiak, M. P., and Karas, B. J., 2020, “Designer Endosymbionts: Converting Free-Living Bacteria into Organelles,” Current Opinion in Systems Biology, 24:41–50.](images/Mindell_Evolu_Fig_7_2.png)

FIGURE 7.2. Potential applications of synthetic mitochondria and chloroplasts (organelles) in research. More efficient photosynthesis and cellular respiration in organisms might be developed using synthesized or improved chloroplasts and mitochondria. Synthetic mitochondria could be used in treatment of human diseases linked to mitochondrial problems. Synthetic organelles could also be used to study biological processes requiring compartmentalization, such as nitrogen fixation. ¶ Diagram modified from Meaney, R. S., Hamadache, S., Soltysiak, M. P., and Karas, B. J., 2020, “Designer Endosymbionts: Converting Free-Living Bacteria into Organelles,” *Current Opinion in Systems Biology*, 24:41–50.

And what if extraterrestrial life already exists? Is the reality of impactful horizontal evolution on Earth truly universal? Would we expect alien life-forms to be an integration among formerly different entities, as I’ve been pointing out for Earthly life? Here too, I think the answer is yes, given the three essential features above. Natural selection can increase (and reduce) the functions and complexity of life-forms over time, and the combination of individuals, whether whole organisms, organismal parts, genomes, or genes, is a tried and true path, over billions of years, to increasing complexity. Just think of yourself, currently with two genomes, a history of interbreeding bouts with other hominid species or lineages, and numerous genes descended from ancient horizontal gene transfer events. The events of lineage joining and endosymbioses outlined earlier are likely to happen among any and all living systems by means of natural selection and chance, given sufficient time.^([29](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-29))

### Outlook

Current analyses all indicate difficult times ahead for Earth’s species. As climates warm and habitats change, as sea levels rise and as wildfires and humans proliferate, species extinctions are increasing. Witnessing losses of biological diversity and natural landscapes are difficult for all who care deeply about the living world. Many feel a moral imperative to prevent extinctions caused by human activities, as inherently wrong. Species are unique life-forms created by natural forces operating over millions of years. Engaging with species in nature connects us to something bigger than ourselves, something both transcendent and ephemeral. We can identify with other species as disparate yet kindred parts of the singular, ancient, and stunning development of life and its history of change. Their histories are a big part of ours. We influence one another, indirectly or directly, and continue to evolve together, as long as we persist. Accepting rights as a human construct, our obligations to other species, ultimately, arise from obligations to ourselves.

However, the view that we must preserve nature as it has been in the recent past lest we risk our own demise is false. It underestimates our capacity to adapt. It also discounts the fact that change in the natural world is inevitable, regardless of causes and outcomes. It would be an act of extreme hubris to suppose we can control a return to earlier conditions and species compositions across the globe. When environmental change is fast, adaptive responses across species, if successful, must also be fast. Journalist Elizabeth Kolbert reviews ways in which we are both changing nature and trying to save it, noting: “The choice is not between what was and what is, but between what is and what will be.”^([30](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-30))

It would also be mistaken to suppose that humans disrupting the natural world is something new. Our ancestors also substantially changed the flora and fauna in the areas they inhabited. As early as two million years ago, *Homo* species have been associated with a two-thirds decline in large African mammals where the mammals’ and their own ranges overlapped. This included reduction of 12 different elephant species to two. During the 100,000 years before 1500 CE, researchers estimate that more than 10 percent of global mammal species were lost to human hunting pressure and habitat modification. Similarly, extinction of over 10 percent of recently existing birds species has been associated with early human occupation of the Pacific islands. Between 3,000 and 10,000 years ago, human hunters contributed to widespread extinctions of the Pleistocene megafauna, including mammoths, mastodons, ground sloths, and saber-toothed cats, as well as dozens of other species in North America and elsewhere. Many of the megafauna species that did survive had their movements, ranges, and population sizes significantly altered. Although most megafauna species were survivors of the previous hundred million years, they were ill prepared for resourceful humans and the environmental changes of the time. Early humans transported and cultivated favored plants in preferred locations, and repeatedly burned large swaths of habitat, restructuring ecosystems. These changes, in turn, influenced the forces of natural selection and the evolution of many other species caught up in the expansion of human populations.^([31](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter7-31))

Conservation efforts remain vital in seeking to minimize losses and retain functioning ecosystems. However, functional, healthy ecosystems may take many forms, and those of the future will differ from those of today, given impending changes in climate and distribution of biological diversity. We don’t know what the long-term impacts of those changes will be. Though few would choose it, there’s little reason to suppose our species could not survive in a world with fewer or different species, depending on which are lost. Taking responsibility and seeking to avoid catastrophic biodiversity change requires collaboration among scientists and policymakers, though past progress in this arena has been limited.

The new evolutionary narrative emphasizes that substantive change can happen quickly via horizontal evolution, as in the many examples presented above. A good part of the success of horizontal evolution’s mechanisms in facilitating adaptive change lies in their decentralization of inheritance. They expand the pool of genetic variation. Rather than being limited to vertical inheritance from immediate parents, some individuals receive new combinations of disparate genetic materials horizontally, creating more and a different scale of variation for selection to act upon.

We already see increases in species hybridizing as changing climate shifts their distributions. Pathogens are unwitting laboratories for recombination and selection as they come into contact with potential new host species, resulting from humans changing environments. There will likely be increased use of genome editing to promote conservation of species and ecosystems. Synthetic biology can be seen as horizontal evolution in human hands, and it will play an increasing role in our future, and because of our dominance, that of other life-forms as well. Decentralized, horizontal inheritance will continue to be a powerful force in evolution’s future narrative, even when humans are gone.

## 8 Future Humans

> We humans are changing. We have become so intertwined with what we have created that we are no longer separate from it.
> 
> —DANNY HILLIS, “BACK TO THE FUTURE (OF 1994)”

Think again about Ötzi the Tyrolean Iceman, our 5,300-year-old predecessor, and see him as the technologist he was. His axe, bow and arrows, cold-weather clothing, snow boots, dried meat from domestic animals, crop grains in his stomach, strings of dry medicinal mushrooms, fire-starter kit, and even his location at a high-elevation mountain pass all bear silent testimony to a culture of language, teaching, learning, and experimentation. The collective knowledge that he and his community applied to their everyday lives was vital. It could be readily shared and increased. Their technology was key to their health, culture, and survival.

These days, technology, or tech, is often associated with the ubiquitous screens of computers and smart devices, and the divisive effects of misinformation on social media. Some see tech as dehumanizing. Private personal information is captured online by scammers and corporate marketing algorithms. Meaningful in-person experiences are declining for some. Young egos can be damaged by anonymous critics and frequent comparison to faked profiles. Tribal passions are easily manipulated. Chat bots readily outperform humans in composing eloquent answers to complex questions, and will detail dark plans for subterfuge or revenge if asked.

Yes, technology can be harmful, as always. However, technology is also a signature feature for humans. In broad terms, technology is any knowledge used to solve problems and invent tools. Integration of technology has accompanied our every step as *Homo sapiens*. It is driven and spread by learning and cultural change, rather than genetic transmission. It is horizontal evolution, as the inheritance is not limited to parents and offspring, yet it can persist through time. Most of us have lost touch with Ötzi’s technology, but our goals in pursuit and application of technology are similar to his: health, security, and gaining knowledge in an uncertain world. Our talent and obsession for developing technology is a big part of our evolutionary success as a species. It is essential to our culture.

In this chapter I extend evolution’s new narrative, focusing on our integration with technology. I’ll explore aspects of technology’s impacts on our bodies, brains, minds, and culture. I’ll make the case that we are, and have long been, in a codependent, coevolutionary relationship with our technology, as learning accelerates our species’ cultural and physical evolution.

### Humans and Technology

Humans are not alone in cultivating technology. Chimpanzees make spears for hunting small mammals. Black kites, an avian predator species, carry and drop burning sticks that spread fires and flush their prey. Some bees dance to communicate to relatives the direction and distance to food. In a processes called quorum sensing, some bacteria, archaea, and viruses use molecular signaling to gauge their population sizes, and manage their behavior and life-cycle change. But we humans are unique in the intentionality, complexity, and speed of change in our technology. At least we like to think so. Since Ötzi’s time we have, at our best, developed technologies for feeding and sheltering billions of people, for reducing disease, for extending life-spans, for fast global communications, and for terrestrial and planetary travel. The transmission and change of human culture by learning are much faster than evolutionary change of organisms by genetics alone. And yet, despite our technological advances since Ötzi’s time, our appearance and physical abilities are still similar to those of his community.

Our human relationship with technology is also a high point for life’s intentional relationship with technology. Life needed about 3.8 billion years to decipher its own DNA coding instructions, to land a probe on Mars, and to make a powerful pocket-sized computer for phoning home, making movies, and trading stocks. Ours is the species that is willfully accelerating its involvement with technology, to the point of dependency. In the absence of key technologies, like electricity, computers, and fossil-fuel refineries, most societies would quickly unravel. No pumps or transport for water delivery. No refrigeration for food. No communications for commerce, travel, health care, or emergency services. What began as convenience has become obligatory—or nearly so.

The role of technology in our lives now and possibly in our species’ future evolution is powerful, and merits our attention. There are many ways to think about technology’s role in human evolution, depending on which features one wants to highlight. But keeping it simple, let’s identify three broad stages: biological, cultural, and technological.^([1](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-1)) The first (biological) stage spans prehuman evolution, including our primate legacy. It concludes with the origins of modern humans, *Homo sapiens*, roughly 300,000 to 350,000 years ago. This estimate is based on fossil skull fragments from Morocco and associated stone scrapers.^([2](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-2)) Evolution during this first stage was primarily biological and genetic. Bodies and behaviors changed over time, influenced by the environment, chance mutations, and natural selection. Our intentional technology development, characterized by the beginnings of language and primitive tool use, was in its very early stages. Estimating times for human language origins is controversial; however, recent studies suggest the origins of speech with contrasting vowel sounds is much older than previously thought. Researchers place spoken language origins among our primate ancestors as far back as 20 million years ago.^([3](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-3)) In this light, humans, as well as some of our cousins, have always been technologists.

A second stage (cultural) corresponds to the time when humans’ nascent technology accelerated their population growth and spread across the planet. This stage, beginning with *Homo sapiens*’ origins and ending in 1500 CE, corresponding roughly with invention of the printing press, is characterized by development of larger societies, agriculture, written language, and printing presses.

A third stage (technological) is now well underway, as our technology allows us to further transcend the limits of our biology. Our bodies and minds can be enhanced in ways both well established and currently being explored. We can augment or replace defective or worn out human body parts ([figure 8.1](#16_ch8.xhtml#fig8-1)). We can restore and even improve our senses of hearing and sight. Our memories are bolstered by online searches and an expanding public knowledge base is curated by Wikipedia and similar resources. We can manipulate our genomes and those of other species. We can and do dramatically change our environments for better and worse. We can enhance our powers of analysis with artificial intelligence (AI), and intentionally create new life-forms or change existing ones, as described in [chapter 7](#15_ch7.xhtml).

 ![FIGURE 8.1. Technology allows us to enhance our bodies, minds, and longevity as we continue to transcend prior biological limits.](images/Mindell_Evolu_Fig_8_1.png)

FIGURE 8.1. Technology allows us to enhance our bodies, minds, and longevity as we continue to transcend prior biological limits.

The knowledge being gained and applied by our species is unique in the 3.8-billion-year history of life on Earth.
