Though complex societies and behaviors such as cooperation in raising young have evolved in other species, including various ants and bees, mole rats, meerkats, and a few birds like scrub jays, the second and third stages (cultural and technological) distinguish us from other life-forms. The trajectory we are on is novel and its course is unpredictable.

We’re in a long-term relationship with technology. We can’t break up, even if we wanted to. Sure, we can forgo some personal conveniences. I’d happily frequent local stores more often and even read physical newspapers if online access disappeared. But our relationship with technology is about much more than convenience. Our institutions of governance, defense, commerce, health care, travel, finance, communications, and learning all depend on technology that even luddites are unlikely to quit. Don’t forget our basic reliance on technology services from utilities and governments for water distribution, electricity, home heating, cooling, refrigeration, and light. A 2021 winter storm in Texas knocked out a major power grid and caused at least 700 deaths in just 42 hours.^([4](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-4)) Our lives and even our civilization are dependent on sustained technology.

### Bionic Bodies and Longer Life-Spans

#### Bodies

The integration of humans and technology, once fantasy, is a growing reality. Edgar Allan Poe wrote a piece in 1850 titled “The Man That Was Used Up” about a fictitious general who was so injured in battle that he required assembly from inert parts. Darth Vader, Iron Man, Terminator, and Terminatrix are all fictional cyborgs (cybernetic organisms), combining humans and machines or machinelike properties. In *The Singularity Is Near*, futurist Ray Kurzweil alludes to hypothetical technology that could one day replace body parts, including software that supersedes the brain. He predicted at the turn of the twenty-first century that a “technology singularity” is approaching, a time when machine intelligence becomes more powerful than human intelligence and technology growth becomes uncontrollable. He attributed these developments to exponential increases in technologies of computer science, information processing, genetics, engineering and robotics.^([5](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-5)) Where are we now in the ongoing integration of humans and technology?

Physicians can replace damaged and painful joints. Hips, knees, ankles, shoulders, and fingers can be rebuilt with prosthetics of titanium, cobalt-chromium, zirconium, nickel, or ceramics. If all goes well, hospital time is just one to a few days (see [figure 8.1](#16_ch8.xhtml#fig8-1)). Costs for these procedures are high, and of course no surgery is without some risk. Nonetheless, their frequency is growing. Usually lasting 15 or more years, over one million hip and knee replacements are done annually in the United States, and this is predicted to expand to four million per year by 2030 as the population ages.

Organ transplants are also being performed with increasing success, especially for kidneys, livers, and hearts. Less common transplants include lungs, pancreas, and intestines. Bone marrow can be transplanted as a means to fight cancers. Blood transfusions are critical following injury and blood loss and in fighting anemia. Vision can be restored by replacing damaged corneas. Repair and replacement of teeth is routine, and in some cases requires bone grafts to strengthen the tooth socket using materials from the recipient, from another person, or even from cows.

Researchers are also developing methods for 3D printing of livers, kidneys, and hearts. These synthetic organs will use the recipient’s own cells to minimize immune system resistance following the replacement. There is promise in 3D printed organs for saving lives of those on the waiting list for receipt of the small number of organs available from donors. These methods are still in development; however, pieces of printed organs are currently in use for testing drugs. We are already using 3D printing with human cells for reconstructing ears and noses for burn and accident victims.

Brain-computer interfaces are in development. Brain implants, often attached to the brain surface through a small hole in the skull, can establish a functioning work-around for brain areas damaged after a stroke or head injury. Other implants stimulate brain regions to relieve symptoms of Parkinson’s disease and depression. There is potential to address issues of paralysis, blindness, and deafness, though these remain hypothetical. Gene therapy seeks to treat diseases by repairing or replacing the underlying genes. This is complicated, especially compared with straightforward joint replacement. One of the reasons is the difficulty of gene delivery and uptake. Another reason is that most diseases have multiple underlying genes, while many genes also have multiple effects. Gene therapy efforts began in the 1980s and have been recently enhanced by the introduction of advanced gene editing tools. So far, only a few therapies have received approval by regulatory agencies. One of these takes immune cells from a patient’s body, manipulates them in a lab to target specific cancer cells, and then returns them to the patient. Another treats an inherited form of blindness, using a virus to deliver a gene, which produces a missing enzyme in the eye, thereby improving the retina and vision. Hundreds more gene therapies are in development, targeting diseases such as blood cancers, skin cancers, and cardiovascular diseases. It seems to be just a matter of persistence until the catalog of addressable diseases grows.

These procedures, however, affect only the individual person having them. Replacement body parts, including genes introduced into body cells such as blood cells, are not passed to subsequent generations. The replacements do not directly impact our evolution. They might have an indirect effect on reproductive rates for individuals and possibly the maintenance of genes that might otherwise be selected against. But this is likely a very small effect, particularly given the low incidence of such procedures.

Gene therapy that could become heritable would target sex cells (eggs or sperm) or embryos. Though possible in theory using CRISPR-Cas9, and attempted as a means to block HIV infection in one controversial case, this raises questions about the ethics of tinkering directly with human design and its potential unintended consequences. Gene therapy with embryos is currently banned in many but not all countries, while international efforts among scientists and government officials seek consensus on regulations. Although reducing heritable disease is desirable, ethical issues loom regarding what heritable traits might be changed, who has access to such treatments, and whether parents have a right to genetically edit their descendants. I will revisit this topic of editing sex cells below.

![FIGURE 8.2. Estimated number of people aged 100 and older worldwide from 2010 to 2100. ¶ Data from Statista—The Statistics Portal, “Number of People Aged 100 and Older (Centenarians) Worldwide from 2000 to 2100,” Demographics, 2023, https://www.statista.com/statistics/996597/number-centenarians-worldwide/.](images/Mindell_Evolu_Fig_8_2.png)

FIGURE 8.2. Estimated number of people aged 100 and older worldwide from 2010 to 2100. ¶ Data from Statista—The Statistics Portal, “Number of People Aged 100 and Older (Centenarians) Worldwide from 2000 to 2100,” Demographics, 2023, [https://www.statista.com/statistics/996597/number-centenarians-worldwide/](https://www.statista.com/statistics/996597/number-centenarians-worldwide/).

#### Life-Spans

Many people are aware that average human life-spans are increasing. Fewer recognize that the maximum age, roughly 125 years, has not changed much since the end of the Stone Age 5,000 years ago.^([6](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-6)) What has changed is life expectancy, the average time that an individual lives, and the frequency with which some reach milestones such as 100 years of age ([figure 8.2](#16_ch8.xhtml#fig8-2)). Longer life expectancy stems from greatly reduced infant mortality, improved hygiene, and public health measures, plus advances in medicine. These are the result, in turn, of improved knowledge and technology regarding healthy lifestyles, genetic markers and monitoring for early warning about diseases, and improvements in medicine. Regarding diet, recent research confirms well-known advice for a longer, healthier life. Restrict calories without malnutrition. Stop eating before feeling stuffed. Eat less often. Fast for 12–16 hours on occasion. Hippocrates knew much of this 2,400 years ago, though knowledge is not application.

Exciting new research into the aging process (senescence), shows it to be driven by a changing balance of DNA damage and repair processes in our bodies over time ([figure 8.3](#16_ch8.xhtml#fig8-3)).^([7](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-7)) The DNAs in all of our cells naturally experience damage over time, as they are copied and exposed to toxins, carcinogens, and radiation. Our cells have impressive innate abilities to repair those damaged DNAs. However, when the rate of damage exceeds the rate of repair, and it does sooner or later, the damage accumulates. This damage contributes to overall senescence, characterized by diminishing levels of DNA repair and reduced capacity to eliminate abnormal cells, part of an important process called apoptosis, or programmed cell death. The outcomes of these problems in DNA repair and apoptosis are increases in the illnesses of old age: cancer, dementia, heart disease, respiratory disease, and others.

Let’s consider a few current research projects on the mechanisms and possible control of aging. Sirtuins are a family of proteins that influence important cell functions. These include DNA repair, healthy inflammation in response to infection, and apoptosis, noted above as the process of eliminating abnormal cells. When apoptosis fails, aging, cancerous, and virus-infected cells can proliferate. Because of their important functions, damage to sirtuins or excessive use and stress on them can accelerate aging. It is not yet clear how to apply this knowledge to slow the aging process in humans. However, stimulation of sirtuin production in mice has led to clear improvements in DNA repair, memory, and physical endurance, which are rough proxies for aging.

![FIGURE 8.3. Summary of the major influences and mechanisms of human aging. ¶ Drawing modified from Melzer, D., Pilling, L. C., and Ferrucci, L., 2020, “The Genetics of Human Ageing,” Nature Reviews Genetics, 21(2):88–101.](images/Mindell_Evolu_Fig_8_3.png)

FIGURE 8.3. Summary of the major influences and mechanisms of human aging. ¶ Drawing modified from Melzer, D., Pilling, L. C., and Ferrucci, L., 2020, “The Genetics of Human Ageing,” *Nature Reviews Genetics*, 21(2):88–101.

mTOR is another group of proteins implicated in aging. Similar to the sirtuins, mTOR also functions in DNA repair and digestion of old proteins in humans. But when mTOR is overexpressed and overly active, its usual functions can be compromised. Studies of nonmammals have found that reducing activity of some of the same proteins—called TOR without the “m” for mammalian—leads to a longer life-span in yeast, roundworms, and fruit flies. In mice, an inhibitor of mTOR, rapamycin, also increases life-span. Trials of a rapamycin-like drug in humans resulted in improvements in immune-system function and fewer infections, but possible effects on longevity are still unknown.^([8](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-8))

Natural biological stresses can damage cells causing unhealthy inflammation, failure to replicate, and, as with sirtuins and mTOR, resistance to apoptosis and the destruction of abnormal cells. Research is underway on drugs that can target and eliminate damaged cells, known as senolytics. Trials in mice have demonstrated an ability for these drugs to reverse several features of aging. Preliminary trials in humans show promise.^([9](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-9)) Based on progress to date, the next few decades are likely to see progress in the knowledge and technologies that reduce DNA damage, enhance DNA repair, and, thereby, slow the processes of aging.

There are many concerns related to increasing average longevity and human population growth. Will the additional years be lived with good health and a sound mind? How will societies, families, and economies adapt to increasing numbers of supercentenarians, the term for those reaching 110 years of age? How will this impact our already heavy consumption of natural resources and the ensuing environmental problems? Will humans stop trying to extend average longevity with good health? I don’t think so. The desire to extend lives will not dissipate as long as people love and care about their families, friends, and others. Further, the scientific challenge of slowing the aging process is irresistible, and, at least incrementally, within reach. We’ve already made good progress (see [figure 8.2](#16_ch8.xhtml#fig8-2)), and that is prior to current understanding of the molecular mechanisms of DNA damage and repair.

Aging and its associated physical and mental decline are, or at least still appear, inevitable. But its pace can be slowed and we seem bound to find the molecular mechanisms responsible. As the saying goes, death is easy, its living that’s hard. Humans are drawn to doing hard things. Often with technology.

### Extending Our Brains

The brain’s ability to coordinate our body functions and movements is impressive. So are our species’ intellectual accomplishments rooted in abstract thought, such as developing the technologies at the heart of this discussion. These accomplishments distinguish our species from the others. However, is your brain optimal? Do you have flawless recall of information and events? Are your judgements unbiased and wise? Are your thoughts reliably creative or well focused? I’m guessing the answers are no. Our brains serve us well in many ways, but as products of evolution they are constrained by our inheritance from early vertebrates, by selection for success in prior environments, and by the materials at hand.

We struggle with the brain’s limits. We forget. We ignore and daydream. We misinterpret and deny. We hope irrationally rather than plan. Our biases, filtered by emotions and personality, are as fascinating as they are humbling.^([10](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-10)) The gambler’s fallacy describes our bias in supposing that future possibilities reflect past events. For example, if you lose nine coin tosses in a row, you feel more than 50 percent likely to win the tenth. You shouldn’t. With groupthink we make irrational decisions to avoid conflict. Sometimes—well, let’s say often—the less we know about a subject, the greater our certainty in our own views about it. This is the Dunning-Kreuger effect. Perhaps most insidious, confirmation bias denotes our tendency to seek and magnify evidence supporting our beliefs and to ignore discordant facts.

At this point, our technology surpasses our brain’s innate abilities alone in many ways. Technology extends our senses in seeing over 13 billion light years away to the furthest known galaxy from Earth. We can detect the movements of electrons, which are 1/1000th the size of protons, which are about 1/25 trillionth of an inch in diameter. Our machines have vastly greater memory and capacity for gathering and analyzing information, boosting our brains in problem solving and prediction.

Using computers to simulate human intelligence in gaining and applying knowledge is deemed artificial intelligence (AI). AI is pervasive in the daily lives of hundreds of millions of people. It is central to most commercial computing and online activity. It’s used in formulating answers to complex questions with Chat GPT, web-search algorithms like Google, recommendation systems as in Facebook and Netflix, targeted advertising, language translation, speech and face recognition, automated asset trading, self-driving cars, weather prediction, navigation, matchmaking, and gaming. Machine learning is a subset of AI in which computers are programmed to use data recursively in improving their analyses. For example, machine learning is at work in identifying junk email and is most effective when given feedback to boost learning. More generally, machine learning provides cycles of reprogramming and improving software based on its own performance.

Experts agree AI’s capacity for rationality and analysis will surpass the intelligence of our brightest individuals, if it hasn’t happened already. Indeed this has been in progress for quite a while, resulting in something called the AI effect. This is when tasks once considered to require intelligence are no longer viewed that way, after AI has solved and automated the job. As cognitive scientist Douglas Hofstadter notes, AI is whatever hasn’t been done yet. Perhaps this is just face-saving, and our way of maintaining our role in the process of solving various problems. Though we may move the goalposts for what qualifies as intelligence, the era of augmenting our brains with software and hardware is making fast progress.

Chat GPT4, freely available to anyone with an internet connection, now has human-level intelligence and could outcompete humans for many jobs. It can write and debug computer code faster than we can, as well as ace standardized tests, including writing essays, for evaluating prospective law, business, and medical students. Many AI experts are urging a pause in further development of “unpredictable black-box models with emergent capabilities,” asking whether we should rush to develop “nonhuman minds that might eventually outnumber, outsmart, obsolete and replace us.”^([11](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-11)) The pause could be used to work out and implement safety protocols that are audited and overseen by independent experts. Efforts to pause or stop technology development have been implemented or attempted with human cloning, human sex-cell editing (as discussed above), and gain-of-function research targeting pathogens; however, those technologies are harder to access than open-access AI apps. Whether or not a pause happens in development of powerful AI systems, and it seems unenforceable and hence unlikely, we should be proactive in seeking and applying wisdom rather than reactive. It will take years to develop the requisite regulatory and governance systems for AI, and in the meantime our existential risk appears real but enigmatic. AI has been compared to technologies of fire, the internet, and nuclear fission for its likely impacts on human lives and society. There have been negative and positive consequences for those, and I suppose the same will be true for AI. It can’t be undeveloped.

Recreating the brain or parts of it with engineered physical devices may contribute to advances, but this approach is handicapped by the legacy of evolutionary history in brain design. This includes its construction using proteins encoded by DNAs. Our brains have brought us far, but natural selection did not optimize them for the kinds of analyses and rationality we often need and that advanced computation can provide.

In the near term, safe self-driving cars, trucks, and buses can be expected to reduce the 1.2 million deaths globally from vehicle accidents each year. A corresponding shift from personal cars to autonomous ride sharing in urban areas can reduce costs of transportation for many, while reducing traffic congestion and space lost to parking. Current virtual assistants like Siri and Alexa are error prone (me: “Alexa, call the police!” Alexa: “Shuffling songs by the Police.”), but improving. Future virtual assistants with enhanced background knowledge and detailed information about their users’ circumstances will be significantly better in managing and integrating calendars, travel bookings, purchases, payments, taxes, investment decisions, healthy meals, relevant unbiased news, and communication with friends and family. If these features were well implemented in a system that could be trusted (no small feat), individuals could be empowered to act more effectively in a complicated and sometimes adverse world.

AI is flowing in the heart of health care. This is based on the growth and use of large datasets in training computer programs to improve themselves and their inferences. This is machine learning. Progress can most readily be seen, perhaps, in those medical specialties that rely on images for making diagnoses. There is an expanding core of comparative imaging data, from MRIs (magnetic resonance imaging), CTs (computed tomography), EKGs (electrocardiograms), ultrasounds, and more. Computers can learn about patterns within the images and link them to the diagnoses that were made by professionals, reviewing hundreds of thousands and eventually millions of patient cases, with a relatively small amount of human oversight. It is still early days for machine learning on medical images, but, in the words of cardiologist and author Eric Topol, this technology has the potential to “diagnose some types of skin cancer as well as or perhaps even better than board-certified dermatologists, to identify specific heart-rhythm abnormalities like cardiologists, to interpret medical scans or pathology slides as well as senior, highly qualified radiologists and pathologists, respectively, to diagnose various eye diseases as well as ophthalmologists, and to predict suicide better than mental health professionals.”^([12](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-12))

Of course, AI will influence more in medicine than just pattern recognition. There is great potential to learn from linking whole genome sequences for millions of patients with particular symptoms and diseases, along with the outcomes of particular treatments. AI-driven computer programs don’t get tired or distracted from overworking. None of this is to say that physicians can or will be replaced. Quite the opposite should be true. As Topol notes, AI computers can support a more humane and empathic medicine, where physicians and other health-care workers have more time for in-person and empathic patient care.

Beyond all these services, AI might be directed to large-scale challenges facing humanity. Computer scientist Stuart Russell notes: “Our intelligence is responsible for our civilization. With access to greater intelligence we could have a greater—and perhaps far better—civilization.” He outlines the challenge of raising the living standard of everyone on Earth, by achieving a tenfold increase in the global GDP (gross domestic product), the production of goods and services per capita, with the help of AI. “The tenfold increase in GDP posited … is predicated not on further revolutionary technologies but on the ability of AI systems to employ what we already have more effectively and at greater scale.”^([13](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-13)) A small group of individuals wanting to build a hospital, school, or waste-treatment facility in their community would have access to all the best knowledge and skills of our species, to be completed with the help of AI-driven design, materials choice, budgeting, logistics, physical robots, including industrial-scale 3D printing, and permitting. The abundance of potential projects when so many are empowered by access to the requisite knowledge represents the sought-after net increase in GDP. Wildly ambitious? Yes. And why not? It’s a worthy goal and can, of course, be approached incrementally. The point is that there is a positive vision for what might be accomplished with AI.

The potential for AI to take a wrong turn, however, seems real. After all, if we create entities with intelligence more powerful than our own, how can we be sure they won’t wield that power over us? Could they develop agency somehow? AI machines will be able to read all the books and manuals ever written before lunch, and digest all the global surveillance-camera videos, phone records, social media, and television and radio broadcasts around the world by dinner. Its understanding of our world and attitudes may outstrip our own. Stuart’s solution is to change the incentives for AI systems. Instead of training AI systems to accomplish a fixed goal, they could be trained, with a technique called inverse reinforcement learning,” to be aligned with human goals, seeking to learn what it is that humans really want and remaining open to being redirected and turned off if need be. This sounds reasonable. Ideally, we can select for this alignment in the adaptive coevolutionary process for humans and technology. But is it foolproof? So much depends on the vagaries of what humans really want. Neither AI nor any other technology is inherently evil. But humans can be, and that seems, as always, to be the greater threat.^([14](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-14))

### Lifelike Technology

Technology has fascinating similarities to living entities. As noted in [chapter 4](#11_ch4.xhtml), for many years life was considered to be characterized by a common set of properties: growth, reproduction, metabolism, heritable features, cellular composition, response to the environment, and homeostasis (the ability to maintain stable internal conditions). Technology has many of those same properties. Machine learning, a branch of AI, can grow and reproduce computer code, responding to changing data about its environment, thereby providing a next-generation algorithm, which is ready for another round of evaluation and change. Metabolism is the way our bodies turn food into energy, and technology can, with human assistance, also produce its own energy by harnessing nuclear reactions, solar and wind power, or fossil-fuel combustion.

Technology is similar, in needing assistance, to life’s many obligate parasites, which are unable to reproduce without resources provided by another species. There are obligate parasites among all the main eukaryote groups, so the phenomenon is not uncommon among life-forms. We can admit that defining life is difficult and arguably subjective because there are ample gray zones, like viruses and plasmids. But we can also admit the circularity of excluding anything from being alive because it’s not biological—in other words, because it’s not already part of life. Revisiting the issue raised in [chapter 4](#11_ch4.xhtml), some philosophers and biologists see life not as discrete physical entities but as processes. These life processes happen at different scales of time and organization, ranging from replication of genes to differentiation of cells, development of organisms, life-cycle changes, speciation, and the mechanisms of horizontal evolution.

For example, rather than seeing the living world as comprising independent species with well delineated boundaries, life would comprise interdependent species having fuzzy boundaries. This view of life as process provides a better fit when considering horizontal evolution, whose processes underlie widespread integration and interaction among species. Dupré and Nicholson note: “The world—at least insofar as living beings are concerned—is made up not of substantial particles or things, as philosophers have overwhelmingly supposed, but of … a hierarchy of processes, stabilized and actively maintained at different timescales.” They point out that the relationships and interdependence among species—think of our relationship with our microbiome—poses a major problem for understanding the natural world as composed of discrete entities rather than processes.^([15](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-15))

Technology, of course, is largely process as it is applied to humans. It is inert prior to application, as code not being run and implants not implanted, and the technology comes to life, metaphorically, when activated in our service, offering analyses, predictions, solutions, and more.

Imagine a single person integrating an extreme degree of technology. Suppose this imaginary person were conceived in vitro, born by cesarian section, recipient of six artificial joints, three metal heart stents, two pig heart valves, a silicon brain implant, half a 3D-printed ear, glasses, two hearing aids, and dentures. Plus, they have been saved on numerous occasions by timely antibiotics, vaccines, insulin injections, blood transfusions, antidepressants, and the interventions of a loving spouse they met online at Match. They were, at the moment of your imagining, listening to a Bach cello suites podcast while flying a helicopter (despite having lost their license for obvious reasons) on instruments through turbulence to a landing pad on an artificial island in the South China Sea, where the helicopter rental fees were paid in bitcoin.

Consider this as the modern extension of human technology going back to Ötzi’s time and earlier, when tool use, language, and agriculture began. It would seem churlish, if not impossible, to deny an obligate dependency on technology, regardless of current lifestyle. Sure, individual humans can forgo much, but not all, technology. In some cases. For a while. But for our species as a whole, our relationship with technology has become both a physical and psychological codependency. Alexa the machine app will never be alive. We reserve that designation, at least for now, for the billions of our cousins, regardless of form, who share evolution and common descent with us. Our technology, however, is increasingly lifelike, and like a true soul mate, it completes us.

There may come a time, futurist and computer scientist Hans Moravec supposes, when individual humans will have immortal avatars.^([16](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-16)) A time when your brain’s 86 billion neurons can be precisely recreated, one by one, as transistors, and appropriately connected to others, in the head of a robot. And as each neuron is recreated, the corresponding neuron of your own is removed. Your brain remains connected to the robot’s, and consciousness is maintained throughout the transfer process. At the end, with your consciousness held by the machine and you feeling everything the robot experiences, you can look back through the robot’s eyes at your former body. The robot, of course, has access to the latest AI in addition to the transistorized brain powering your consciousness.

This begs the question, is the avatar actually you? Keep in mind that the avatar will have your exact memories, habits, and personality. So, answers will vary depending on how you are defined.

### Our Coevolution

Our technology touches every aspect and every moment of our lives. And, yet, it has a kind of invisibility. We grow accustomed to the earthly miracles. A comedian describes people complaining about a delayed airplane drink service while “sitting in a chair—in the sky!” “Flying like a bird!”^([17](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-17)) We see most clearly the newest technologies. We push their limits, and show them to friends. Then we wait impatiently for the next versions, with a growing sense of deprivation. We forget the agricultural revolution behind the little bag of pretzels.

The reality is that our technology has a been primary influence on human cultural evolution for millennia. And the pace is quickening. We shape our technology and our technology shapes our culture and our minds. Technologies can be shared, horizontally, and applied by distant relatives or populations, without the need for physical transfer of any genes. This technology transfer among human populations and individuals, who can then change and adapt the technology, is a form of coevolution and, hence, a form of horizontal evolution.

I discussed coevolution among species earlier, in which different species influence genetic change in each other, as between predators and prey. In the coevolution between technology and humans the mutual influence occurs not between different species but between our species and our technology. Visible or not, technology pervades our culture and is essential to our varied ways of life. Though the mechanisms and time scales differ, both humans and technology evolve over time. We inhabit a rich human cultural environment in addition to the natural environment that envelops all life.

Cultural, technological change can be favored by natural selection if it improves reproductive success of individual humans. It can also be favored when it is either preferred by individuals or amplified through chance events. Our capacity for developing and adapting culture and technology has been a key feature of our evolutionary success as a species. In many ways, culture is our operating system. Evolutionary biologist Kevin Laland notes: “The human cultural capability did not evolve in isolation but in intricate coevolution with central aspects of cognition and behavior, including our language, teaching, intelligence, perspective-taking, powers of computation, cooperative tendencies, tool use, memory and control of the environment.” Biologists have described a positive feedback loop called “cultural drive,” in which greater cognitive ability, especially social learning and transmission of adaptive behaviors, can accelerate biological evolution. In simple form, this feedback loop can be described as: improved cognition → more behavioral innovation → richer culture → more skills to be learned → new cognitive challenges → further improvements in cognition and innovation.^([18](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-18))

Accelerated biological evolution is reflected in the increasing brain volume (both absolute and relative to body weight) in our lineage over the past 3.2 million years. Brain volume expanded approximately threefold from 450 cm³ in Lucy, the fossil *Australopithecus afarensis*, to roughly 1,400 cm³ for the past 15,000 years in humans.^([19](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-19)) A variety of other physical changes in humans have also been linked to cultural change. An evolved tolerance to cow”s milk in adults started about 10,000 years ago on the heels of livestock domestication. Loss of dark skin pigments enhanced production of vitamin D from sunshine, following movements from Africa to darker and colder northern climates where individuals had less sun exposure. More recently, average height across populations has increased by about 10 centimeters and life-spans by 20 years, in association with better nutrition and hygiene. Adaptations have enabled more efficient oxygen metabolism in small human populations settling at high altitudes where oxygen is less abundant, in Tibet, Ethiopia, and the Andes Mountains.

We are poised to give genes a much larger role in our future evolution, depending on our choices and success in pursuing technologies. Thousands of gene therapy trials are already underway. Though most aim to improve health, the methods have potential to enhance other traits, such as size, strength, and intelligence. Our technology for traveling and living beyond Earth also has potential to impact our biological evolution. Extraterrestrial environments with reduced gravity and increased radiation would impose their own selection pressures, and discussion has begun regarding possible human enhancement for future space missions by gene editing and implants.^([20](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-20))

Human reproductive rates and success are relatively less dependent on genes than in the past, and more attuned to local cultural trends and economics. Despite some linkage as noted above, technological change is happening much faster than physical human evolution, which requires many generations. Consider a few technology changes that have developed while humans have aged but have not evolved physically. Karl Benz patented the first car in 1886, humans landed on the Moon in 1969, and space tourism by private companies is well underway in 2023. Global farm productivity for wheat, rice, and maize increased by 214, 150, and 205 percent respectively between 1960 and 2018,^([21](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-21)) and this was at least partly owing to improvements in seed genetics such as greater resistance to insect pests, livestock genetics, equipment, and weather prediction. Smartphones appeared in 1992, and by 2023, 84 percent of the global population are estimated to be users. Facebook launched in 2004, and now about 40 percent of humanity uses social media at least occasionally. Rapid technological changes appear decoupled from physical evolution. At least for now.

Although the long-term biological impact of exponential growth in technology is unknown, our symbiotic relationship with technology is irreversible. Human intellect and subsequent technology, despite their shortcomings, are integral to both our evolutionary past and future. We rightly seek better technologies to minimize their environmental damage and resource use, but few if any prefer a world without medicines or electricity.

Newer technologies will continue to disrupt older ones, as the world population of humans expands from 7.9 billion in 2023 to a predicted 9.7 billion in 2050. Unprecedented energy demand is putting a premium on developing new sustainable sources, as well as more efficient technologies for existing ones. And this demand will grow. Digital health-care services are increasing, including virtual emergency room and doctor consultations, remote monitoring of health data from wireless devices, and rapid sharing of digital records such as MRI scans among specialists and clinics. Genetic testing services from private companies such as Invitae aim to analyze your genes to identify your specific potential for various hereditary cancers, heart conditions, neurological disorders, and reproductive health problems. This information can lead to early intervention and lifestyle changes to prevent disease.

Citizens’ education can benefit from the growing availability of free online course platforms like Kahn Academy and Coursera, as well as electronic texts and learning podcasts. This potential for education does require students’ effort, and an ability to distinguish reliable information from misinformation. However, as costs of higher education soar, online educational resources and options are gaining in popularity and efficiency. Technology-driven changes in robotics, manufacturing, sales, shipping, and banking are all having profound effects on labor markets, as automation and digitization shift labor demand from traditional to more specialized analytical, technical, and managerial skills.

Technology in the form of dating apps is used with growing frequency in our choice of partners and spouses. Falling in love seems beyond our control, but we can improve the likelihood of finding a loving relationship with AI preselecting the candidates based, unromantically, on their multiple-choice answers to ordinary questions about lifestyle and politics. A 2020 Pew Research Center study of 4,860 US adults found that 48 percent of 18–29-year-olds and 38 percent of 30–49-year-olds had used a dating site or app at some point. Further, 23 percent of all adults had dated and 12 percent had married or become committed to someone first met through a dating app.^([22](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-22))

Technology has always been a double-edged sword, and its negative impacts are all too real. Development of gunpowder began in the eighth century BCE with Chinese alchemists seeking an elixir for health and immortality. But heating mixtures of sulfur, saltpeter, and herbs gave rise to smoke then flames and then guns, rather than eternal life. In modern times, development of weapons technology has fostered both wartime destruction and civil benefits. Ballistic missile technology enabled the launch of networked satellites and space exploration. Nuclear technology has spawned nuclear power and nuclear medicine, such as organ imaging techniques. As noted by futurists Alvin and Heidi Toffler, “the way we make war reflects the way we make wealth.”^([23](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-23))

Modern diets favoring processed foods high in sugar and vegetable oils are associated with increasing levels of obesity, diabetes, and heart disease. Being sedentary and spending excessive time on social media are correlated with depression and anxiety, particularly among adolescents. Disinformation campaigns on social media using software bots amplify our worst instincts and biases, casting shade on objective truths and leaving communities angry and divided. Despite the problems, letting go of technology has rarely been our solution. Rather, we seek relief with calls for better technologies and greater wisdom in using them.

What are the mechanisms of our coevolution with technology? Are they different from those sustaining all of life’s evolution? The mechanisms are indeed much the same. However, in our coevolution with technology, humans are primary agents of selection, shaping technology in a way analogous to our shaping of domesticated animals and plants. We create and choose to propagate some forms over others based on our preferences. The abacus gives way to electronic calculators. Flint and steel for cookfires yield to electromagnetism for induction stovetops. The random mutations, for example, that flower breeders might select to improve looks or fragrance are replaced by intentional, directed changes in software and hardware.

Our coevolution with technology also differs from traditional coevolution in that one of the coevolving participants, technology, is only lifelike rather than alive. But this is the nature of cultural evolution. Natural selection among organisms favors change that increases reproductive success, both inside and outside of coevolutionary relationships. Technology does not reproduce in the way that organisms do. However, persistence of technologies through time provides a reasonable analogy for their reproductive success. Those technologies whose iterations persist are those that continue to evolve, as selected by people. Examples of technology persistence are everywhere, from particular spoken languages to AI and powered flight.

There is another factor supporting the concept of our coevolution and integration with technology. That factor is the mutual influence and integrated nature of genes, organisms, and environments. They are not entirely separate entities when you think about their respective activities. Their mutual influence can be seen in the development of an embryo. Change in the embryo’s cellular environment can change expression of genes, which can change the embryo’s form. This mutual influence of genes, organisms, and environment is a perennially hot topic, often framed, in error, as nature versus nurture. This is now well known to be a false dichotomy. Nature *and* nurture is more accurate. Hence the mutual influence noted above.

Let’s try the same formulation for humans and technology as for embryo development. Change in our cultural and technological environment can change selection pressure on our genetic variation, which can change our bodies and brains. Precisely how and why this happens will remain controversial, as relevant data accumulate. Geneticist Richard Lewontin made this general point in *The Triple Helix*: “Organisms and environment influence each other, setting up a feedback loop. Organisms can impact their external environments which then return the impact on the organism. Organisms and environment can both serve as causes and as effects in a coevolutionary process.”^([24](#17_notes.xhtml#chapter8-24))

The capacity for organisms to change and be changed by their environment is as old as life. Life added oxygen to the atmosphere, catalyzing the evolution of air-breathing forms including you and me. Fungus-farming ants grow their own food, facilitating colony life and the evolution of societies with different body forms for individuals specialized for different tasks. Macaws and other birds eat fruits, pass the seeds and, in that way, disperse the plants on which they feed. Beavers are the classic example, building dams to create ponds, and eventually wet meadows, which influence their own form and behavior through selection. Beavers have ripple effects too, changing the environment for wetland plants, fish, and osprey, among others.

Perhaps the most extreme form of our coevolution with technology would be editing of our sex cells (eggs and sperm), and thereby directing our own future evolution. As noted earlier, we have the capacity with CRISPR-Cas9, though the practice is banned in many countries. We do already direct our own evolution in a soft manner, by choosing mates whose appearances, personalities, behaviors, and health appeal to us. We also already screen embryos for genetic diseases. But supposing for the moment that editing of sex cells were pursued unchecked, we could alter the genetic heritage of humans. Many, including political scientist Francis Fukuyama, argue this is among the world’s most dangerous ideas, likely to increase inequality among people and undermine democracy. Coupled with colonizing other planets and sufficient time, it could also lead to effectively new forms or new human species, whose genetics might be tailored to life in different parts of the solar system with different atmospheres, temperatures, and gravity.

This is just science fiction. But imagine that future humans disperse and become interstellar voyagers and denizens. Doing so would be a natural extension of our incessant technological innovation as well as our exploration and dispersal activities across the face of Earth over the past 200,000 years. It then becomes conceivable that intelligent beings, descended from life on Earth (ourselves and perhaps other species), could evolve into a great diversity of forms, carrying life throughout the galaxy. In this fictional though long-term and evolutionary view, we are not now in the last chapter of humans’ or of life’s reign, but on the cusp of new opportunity.

Technology suffuses our lives—and our possible futures. It always has. Humans have never existed without technology. At this point, our lifestyles, population sizes, and distribution are influenced more strongly by technology and politics than by natural selection operating on the genetic variation within our species. Technologies for distribution of clean water and electricity have done more to promote human health and population growth in modern times than any particular genetic innovation.

Of course, our genetics remain fundamental to our existence and basic reproductive success. We embody a marvelous legacy of evolution, stretching back to life’s origin. That legacy enables our athletics, our art, and our intellect. However, the ancient legacy brings restrictions. How could it be otherwise? We have what appear, in hindsight, to be design flaws. Birth canals are narrow relative to the large heads of infants. The tube draining the urinary bladder runs through the prostate gland in males, which swells with age. Our genomes are littered with the detritus of past viral infections and DNA copying mistakes. Mutations themselves, though they empower evolutionary innovation, are undirected and mostly harmful or neutral in their effects. Our emotions, though vital, often cloud our judgement and can lead to debilitating depression.

Our technology, at its best, offers powers once reserved for science fiction. We can refurbish our bodies, extend our brains, extend life-spans, edit our genetic instructions, create novel life-forms, travel and possibly live beyond Earth. It is now possible that future change in the human genome may owe more to genetic engineering than to random mutation and natural selection. The tools are improving, though discussions and consensus on ethics and best practices are lagging. We don’t know how the environmental changes from technology might impact our future physical evolution, but technology’s considerable influence on our cultural and lifestyle evolution is clear.

Technologies driving our cultural evolution are shared and transferred horizontally among humans, explaining their rapid spread. We create, change, and disburse the technologies that in turn color and compose our cultural environment. Even as we struggle to control our technology, it enhances our power and influence among life-forms.

Knowledge and technology as our cultural inheritance provide the final example, focused on humans, of the important role of horizontal evolution in life’s evolution. The mechanisms of hybridization and recombination among species, endosymbioses, horizontal gene transfer, coevolution, and cultural inheritance all support an overarching narrative for evolution with new emphasis on the pattern of mutual influence and integration radiating across life-forms.

14.  [14](#11_ch4.xhtml#chapter4_14). Meyer-Abich introduced the concept of holobionts (*holobionten*) to denote the entity made up of symbiotic partners (Meyer-Abich, A., 1950, *Beiträge zur Theorie der Evolution der Organismen*. *II. Typensynthese durch Holobiose* \[Contributions to the evolutionary theory of organisms, II: Synthesis of types through holobiosis\], Bibliotheca Biotheoretica 5, Leiden: E. J. Brill; Baedke, J., Fábregas-Tejeda, A., and Nieves Delgado, A., 2020, “The Holobiont Concept before Margulis,” *Journal of Experimental Zoology B*, 334(3):149–55). This term and the concept of the integrated, composite nature of most species was neglected until the 1990s (see Margulis, L., 1991, “Symbiogenesis and Symbionticism,” in *Symbiosis as a Source of Evolutionary Innovation: Speciation and Morphogenesis*, edited by Margulis, L. and Fester, R., 1–14, Cambridge, MA: MIT Press; Mindell, D. P., 1992, “Phylogenetic Consequences of Symbioses: Eukarya and Eubacteria Are Not Monophyletic Taxa,” *Biosystems*, 27:53–62). Subsequently, the term “holobiont” has become more restricted, and is usually applied to a host and all of its symbiotic microorganisms, potentially including bacteria, archaea, eukaryotic microorganisms, and viruses (Rohwer, F., Seguritan, V., Azam, F., and Knowlton, N., 2002, “Diversity and Distribution of Coral-Associated Bacteria,” *Marine Ecology Progress Series*, 243:1–10; Zilber-Rosenberg, I., and Rosenberg, E., 2021, “Microbial-Driven Genetic Variation in Holobionts,” *FEMS Microbiology Reviews*, 45(6):p.fuab022).
     
15.  [15](#11_ch4.xhtml#chapter4_15). Nyholm, S. V., and McFall-Ngai, M. J., 2021, “A Lasting Symbiosis: How the Hawaiian Bobtail Squid Finds and Keeps Its Bioluminescent Bacterial Partner,” *Nature Reviews Microbiology*, 19(10):666–79.

## Chapter 8

1.  [1](#16_ch8.xhtml#chapter8_1). Others have designated stages for human evolution in similar fashion. K. N. Laland (2017, *Darwin’s Unfinished Symphony*, Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press) has identified human evolutionary stages as genetic, genetic-cultural, and cultural. M. Tegmark (2018, *Life 3.0: Being Human in the Age of Artificial Intelligence*, New York: Vintage Books) has identified Life 1.0, 2.0, and 3.0 corresponding to (1) life evolving its hardware and software, (2) life evolving its hardware but designing much of its software via culture, and (3) life designing its hardware and software via technology. E. A. Lee (2020, *The Coevolution: The Entwined Futures of Humans and Machines*, Cambridge, MA: MIT Press) adds a fourth “synthetic” stage to Laland’s scenario, which is characterized by the emergence of a new life-form based on silicon rather than carbon.
    
2.  [2](#16_ch8.xhtml#chapter8_2). Hublin, J. J., Ben-Ncer, A., Bailey, S. E., Freidline, S. E., Neubauer, S., Skinner, M. E., Amani, F., Rué, M., Fernandes, P., Raynal, J. P., Geraads, D., Ben-Ncer, A., and Hublin, J.
